{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"event","params":{"type":"ToolResult","payload":{"tool_call_id":"functions.ReadFile:2","return_value":{"is_error":false,"output":"     1\t# Domain Background: Number Theory Proof Evaluation\n     2\t\n     3\tThis reference covers the mathematical theory, common proof techniques, and evaluation criteria relevant to olympiad number theory problems. Sub-agents auditing **proof_01**, **proof_06**, **proof_10**, and **proof_17** should read this document before evaluating their assigned proof.\n     4\t\n     5\t---\n     6\t\n     7\t## Part I: Divisibility and Primes\n     8\t\n     9\t### 1.1 Fundamental Theorem of Arithmetic\n    10\t\n    11\tEvery integer n > 1 has a unique prime factorization n = p₁^{a₁} · p₂^{a₂} · ... · pₖ^{aₖ} with p₁ < p₂ < ... < pₖ primes and each aᵢ ≥ 1.\n    12\t\n    13\t**Proof sketch.** Existence follows from strong induction: if n is not prime, write n = ab with 1 < a,b < n and apply induction to a and b. Uniqueness follows from Euclid's lemma: if p is prime and p | ab, then p | a or p | b. This prevents cancellation of distinct prime factors.\n    14\t\n    15\t**Common errors:** Proofs that use unique factorization without explicitly invoking Euclid's lemma, treating the uniqueness as \"obvious,\" will have a logical gap if the problem's context does not make unique factorization automatic.\n    16\t\n    17\t### 1.2 Greatest Common Divisor and Bézout's Identity\n    18\t\n    19\tFor integers a and b not both zero, gcd(a, b) is the largest positive integer dividing both. Bézout's identity states: there exist integers x, y such that ax + by = gcd(a, b). The extended Euclidean algorithm computes these coefficients explicitly.\n    20\t\n    21\t**Key consequence:** gcd(a, b) = 1 if and only if there exist x, y with ax + by = 1, i.e., a has a multiplicative inverse mod b.\n    22\t\n    23\t**Evaluation note:** When a proof claims \"since gcd(a,b)=1, we may invert a modulo b,\" this is valid only if the proof has already established or been given that gcd(a,b)=1. A proof that assumes coprimality without proof commits an unjustified hypothesis error.\n    24\t\n    25\t### 1.3 Modular Arithmetic and Congruence Classes\n    26\t\n    27\tFor a fixed modulus m > 0, the integers are partitioned into m residue classes {0, 1, ..., m-1}. Addition and multiplication are compatible with this equivalence: if a ≡ a' (mod m) and b ≡ b' (mod m) then a+b ≡ a'+b' and ab ≡ a'b' (mod m).\n    28\t\n    29\t**Modular inverse:** The inverse of a modulo m exists if and only if gcd(a, m) = 1. When m = p is prime, every nonzero element has a unique inverse in {1, ..., p-1}.\n    30\t\n    31\t**Fermat's Little Theorem.** If p is prime and p ∤ a, then a^{p-1} ≡ 1 (mod p).\n    32\t\n    33\t**Proof.** The map x ↦ ax is a bijection on {1, ..., p-1} (since gcd(a,p)=1). Therefore a·2a·3a···(p-1)a ≡ 1·2·3···(p-1) (mod p), giving a^{p-1}(p-1)! ≡ (p-1)! (mod p). Since gcd((p-1)!, p) = 1, we may divide both sides by (p-1)! to get a^{p-1} ≡ 1. □\n    34\t\n    35\t**Euler's Theorem.** For gcd(a, m) = 1, a^{φ(m)} ≡ 1 (mod m), where φ(m) = |{1 ≤ k ≤ m : gcd(k,m)=1}| is Euler's totient function.\n    36\t\n    37\t### 1.4 Wilson's Theorem\n    38\t\n    39\t**Theorem (Wilson, 1770).** An integer p > 1 is prime if and only if (p-1)! ≡ -1 (mod p).\n    40\t\n    41\t**Proof (⇒).** Let p be prime. Consider the multiplicative group G = (Z/pZ)*, which has order p-1. Each element a ∈ G has a unique inverse a⁻¹ ∈ G. The equation a² ≡ 1 (mod p) means p | (a-1)(a+1), so by primality p | a-1 or p | a+1, giving a ≡ ±1 (mod p). Therefore in G, only 1 and p-1 are self-inverse. Pairing all other elements {2, 3, ..., p-2} with their distinct inverses, the product of each pair is 1. Hence (p-1)! ≡ 1·(p-1)·1 ≡ -1 (mod p). □\n    42\t\n    43\t**Proof (⇐).** Suppose (n-1)! ≡ -1 (mod n). If n = ab with 1 < a,b < n, then a | (n-1)! and a | n, so a | 1. Contradiction. Hence n is prime. □\n    44\t\n    45\t**Generalization.** For prime p and integer r with 1 ≤ r ≤ p-1:\n    46\t(p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)···(p-r) · (p-r-1)!\n    47\t\n    48\tThe first factor satisfies:\n    49\t(p-1)(p-2)···(p-r) ≡ (-1)(-2)···(-r) = (-1)^r · r! (mod p)\n    50\t\n    51\tTherefore (p-1)! ≡ (-1)^r · r! · (p-r-1)! (mod p), and since (p-1)! ≡ -1 (mod p):\n    52\t-1 ≡ (-1)^r · r! · (p-r-1)! (mod p)\n    53\t(p-r-1)! ≡ (-1)^{r+1} / r! ≡ (-1)^{r+1} · (r!)^{-1} (mod p)\n    54\t\n    55\t**Applications to factorial-divisibility:** For a polynomial f(x) = x - r (r a positive integer), we have f(p) = p - r for prime p > r, so f(p)! = (p-r)!. Wilson's theorem then determines 2·(p-r)! + 1 mod p.\n    56\t\n    57\t**Evaluation criteria for Wilson-theorem proofs:**\n    58\t- Correct: applying Wilson's theorem to derive (p-r)! ≡ c (mod p) for explicit constant c\n    59\t- Incorrect: applying Wilson's theorem to composite moduli\n    60\t- Incorrect: computing (p-1)! = (p-r)! · (p-r)(p-r+1)···(p-1) and claiming (p-r)(p-r+1)···(p-1) ≡ (-r)(-r+1)···(-1) = (-1)^r · r! without justification (this step IS valid mod p but should be stated)\n    61\t- Gap: failing to verify that the constant D_r = 2(-1)^{r+1} + r! is indeed zero only at specific r values\n    62\t\n    63\t### 1.5 Lifting the Exponent Lemma\n    64\t\n    65\tFor odd prime p and p ∤ a, p ∤ b with p | a-b:\n    66\tv_p(a^n - b^n) = v_p(a - b) + v_p(n)\n    67\t\n    68\twhere v_p(m) denotes the p-adic valuation of m (the exponent of p in the prime factorization of m).\n    69\t\n    70\tFor p = 2 and 2 | a-b:\n    71\tv_2(a^n - b^n) = v_2(a-b) + v_2(a+b) + v_2(n) - 1\n    72\t\n    73\t**Common errors involving LTE:** Applying LTE without first checking that the prime p divides a-b (but not a or b). Using LTE for p = 2 without the corrected formula.\n    74\t\n    75\t---\n    76\t\n    77\t## Part II: Gaussian Integers and Algebraic Number Theory\n    78\t\n    79\t### 2.1 The Gaussian Integers Z[i]\n    80\t\n    81\tThe Gaussian integers are Z[i] = {a + bi : a, b ∈ Z} with the usual complex multiplication. Key properties:\n    82\t\n    83\t**Norm:** N(a + bi) = a² + b² = (a+bi)(a-bi). The norm is multiplicative: N(αβ) = N(α)N(β).\n    84\t\n    85\t**Units:** The units of Z[i] are exactly the elements with norm 1: {±1, ±i}.\n    86\t\n    87\t**Gaussian primes:** An element π ∈ Z[i] \\ {0} is a Gaussian prime if π = αβ implies α or β is a unit. The Gaussian primes are:\n    88\t- (1+i) and its associates (since N(1+i) = 2)\n    89\t- p + 0i for rational primes p ≡ 3 (mod 4) (inert primes)\n    90\t- a + bi where a² + b² = p for rational primes p ≡ 1 (mod 4) and p = 2 (split primes)\n    91\t\n    92\t**Unique factorization:** Z[i] is a Euclidean domain (division algorithm with Euclidean function N), hence a PID and UFD. Every nonzero non-unit has a factorization into Gaussian primes, unique up to units.\n    93\t\n    94\t### 2.2 Factoring Integers in Z[i]\n    95\t\n    96\tFor a rational prime p:\n    97\t- If p ≡ 3 (mod 4): p remains prime in Z[i] (p is a Gaussian prime)\n    98\t- If p ≡ 1 (mod 4): p = π·π̄ where π = a + bi with a² + b² = p (by Fermat's theorem on sums of two squares)\n    99\t- If p = 2: 2 = -i(1+i)² (ramified prime, associated to (1+i)²)\n   100\t\n   101\t**Sum of two squares identity:** (a² + b²)(c² + d²) = (ac-bd)² + (ad+bc)² = (ac+bd)² + (ad-bc)². This follows from N(αβ) = N(α)N(β) in Z[i].\n   102\t\n   103\t### 2.3 The Identity X⁴ + Y⁴ in Z[i]\n   104\t\n   105\tThe factorization X⁴ + Y⁴ = (X² + iY²)(X² - iY²) = (X²+iY²)·\\overline{(X²+iY²)} is fundamental to many olympiad problems involving fourth powers.\n   106\t\n   107\t**Key point:** X² + iY² and X² - iY² are complex conjugates. Their product is X⁴ + Y⁴ ∈ Z.\n   108\t\n   109\t**Coprimality condition:** gcd(X²+iY², X²-iY²) in Z[i] divides their difference 2iY² and their sum 2X². If gcd(X,Y) = 1 in Z, one must still carefully analyze which Gaussian primes can divide both X²+iY² and X²-iY².\n   110\t\n   111\t**UFD consequence:** If X²+iY² and X²-iY² are coprime in Z[i] and their product = α⁵ for some α ∈ Z[i], then each factor must itself be a fifth power (times a unit) in Z[i].\n   112\t\n   113\t**Critical subtlety:** Establishing coprimality of X²+iY² and X²-iY² requires checking that no Gaussian prime divides both. A common approach: any common divisor γ divides both (X²+iY²)+(X²-iY²) = 2X² and (X²+iY²)-(X²-iY²) = 2iY². If γ is not an associate of (1+i), then γ | X² and γ | Y², which combined with gcd(X,Y)=1 in Z gives a contradiction.\n   114\t\n   115\t**Evaluation criteria for Gaussian integer proofs:**\n   116\t- Must explicitly verify coprimality of the two conjugate factors, not just assert it\n   117\t- Must correctly enumerate all units in Z[i] (not just ±1)\n   118\t- Must carefully handle the case of the ramified prime (1+i)\n   119\t- Conclusions about when two expressions \"must be fifth powers\" require verified coprimality + UFD\n   120\t- The claim \"since they are coprime and their product is an nth power, each is an nth power (times a unit)\" is valid in any UFD and may be stated without proof in an olympiad context\n   121\t\n   122\t### 2.4 Quadratic Residues and the Legendre Symbol\n   123\t\n   124\tFor odd prime p and integer a with p ∤ a, a is a **quadratic residue** modulo p if there exists x with x² ≡ a (mod p).\n   125\t\n   126\t**Legendre symbol:** (a/p) = 1 if a is a QR mod p, -1 if not, 0 if p | a.\n   127\t\n   128\t**Euler's criterion:** (a/p) ≡ a^{(p-1)/2} (mod p).\n   129\t\n   130\t**Quadratic reciprocity (Gauss):** For distinct odd primes p, q:\n   131\t(p/q)(q/p) = (-1)^{(p-1)(q-1)/4}\n   132\t\n   133\tSupplements: (-1/p) = (-1)^{(p-1)/2} and (2/p) = (-1)^{(p²-1)/8}.\n   134\t\n   135\t**Applications:** Quadratic residues determine which integers are represented by binary quadratic forms. The form x² + y² represents n if and only if every prime factor p ≡ 3 (mod 4) appears to an even power in n. This is equivalent to the Gaussian integer factorization of n having no inert prime factors to odd powers.\n   136\t\n   137\t---\n   138\t\n   139\t## Part III: Diophantine Equations and p-adic Methods\n   140\t\n   141\t### 3.1 Linear Diophantine Equations\n   142\t\n   143\tax + by = c has integer solutions if and only if gcd(a,b) | c. When solutions exist, the general solution is x = x₀ + (b/d)t, y = y₀ - (a/d)t for t ∈ Z, where d = gcd(a,b) and (x₀,y₀) is any particular solution.\n   144\t\n   145\t### 3.2 Pythagorean Triples\n   146\t\n   147\tInteger solutions to x² + y² = z² with gcd(x,y,z) = 1 and x,z > 0, y even are:\n   148\tx = m² - n², y = 2mn, z = m² + n²\n   149\tfor positive integers m > n with gcd(m,n) = 1 and m ≢ n (mod 2).\n   150\t\n   151\t### 3.3 Fermat's Last Theorem Context\n   152\t\n   153\tFor n ≥ 3, the equation xⁿ + yⁿ = zⁿ has no solutions in positive integers. The proof for n = 4 (Fermat himself) and n = 3 (Euler) uses infinite descent. The general case is Wiles's theorem (1995). In olympiad contexts, fourth-power problems often use Gaussian integer factorizations.\n   154\t\n   155\t### 3.4 Valuation Theory\n   156\t\n   157\tThe p-adic valuation v_p(n) is the exponent of p in the prime factorization of n. Properties:\n   158\t- v_p(ab) = v_p(a) + v_p(b)\n   159\t- v_p(a+b) ≥ min(v_p(a), v_p(b)) with equality if v_p(a) ≠ v_p(b)\n   160\t- v_p(a^n) = n · v_p(a)\n   161\t\n   162\t**Divisibility by prime powers:** n | m if and only if v_p(n) ≤ v_p(m) for all primes p.\n   163\t\n   164\t**Common error:** Using v_p(a+b) = min(v_p(a), v_p(b)) when v_p(a) = v_p(b). In this case, v_p(a+b) could be strictly greater than min(v_p(a), v_p(b)).\n   165\t\n   166\t---\n   167\t\n   168\t## Part IV: Multiplicative Functions and Arithmetic Functions\n   169\t\n   170\t### 4.1 The Euler Totient Function\n   171\t\n   172\tφ(n) = |{1 ≤ k ≤ n : gcd(k,n)=1}|. Key properties:\n   173\t- φ(p) = p-1 for prime p\n   174\t- φ(p^k) = p^k - p^{k-1} = p^{k-1}(p-1) for prime power p^k\n   175\t- φ is multiplicative: gcd(m,n)=1 implies φ(mn) = φ(m)φ(n)\n   176\t- Σ_{d|n} φ(d) = n\n   177\t\n   178\t### 4.2 The Number and Sum of Divisors\n   179\t\n   180\tτ(n) = number of divisors of n, σ(n) = sum of divisors of n. Both are multiplicative. For prime power p^k: τ(p^k) = k+1, σ(p^k) = (p^{k+1}-1)/(p-1).\n   181\t\n   182\t### 4.3 Mobius Function and Inversion\n   183\t\n   184\tThe Möbius function: μ(1) = 1, μ(n) = (-1)^k if n is a product of k distinct primes, μ(n) = 0 if p² | n for some prime p.\n   185\t\n   186\t**Möbius inversion formula:** If f(n) = Σ_{d|n} g(d) then g(n) = Σ_{d|n} μ(n/d) f(d).\n   187\t\n   188\t---\n   189\t\n   190\t## Part V: Prime Distribution and Dirichlet's Theorem\n   191\t\n   192\t### 5.1 Dirichlet's Theorem on Primes in Arithmetic Progressions\n   193\t\n   194\tFor gcd(a,d) = 1, the arithmetic progression {a, a+d, a+2d, ...} contains infinitely many primes. Moreover, the primes are equidistributed among the φ(d) coprime residue classes modulo d.\n   195\t\n   196\t**Consequence:** If a property holds for all primes p > N, and it is asserted that only finitely many primes can satisfy a given fixed congruence condition, this requires careful use of Dirichlet's theorem or direct analysis of the fixed integer whose prime factors are at issue.\n   197\t\n   198\t**Key application:** If D is a fixed nonzero integer and we need p | D for all primes p in some infinite set, then D must be 0. This is because any nonzero integer has finitely many prime divisors. This principle is heavily used in the polynomial-prime divisibility problems.\n   199\t\n   200\t### 5.2 Chebyshev's Theorem\n   201\t\n   202\tThere is always a prime between n and 2n for n ≥ 1. This is weaker than PNT but sufficient for many olympiad applications.\n   203\t\n   204\t---\n   205\t\n   206\t## Part VI: Common Proof Structures in Number Theory Problems\n   207\t\n   208\t### 6.1 Infinite Descent\n   209\t\n   210\tUsed to show Diophantine equations have no positive integer solutions. Assume a minimal positive solution exists and derive a smaller solution, contradicting minimality.\n   211\t\n   212\t**Evaluation criterion:** The descent step must produce a strictly smaller (by well-ordering) solution. A proof that claims \"we can repeat this process\" without explicit minimality or termination is incomplete.\n   213\t\n   214\t### 6.2 Polynomial Congruences\n   215\t\n   216\tFor a polynomial f(x) ∈ Z[x] of degree d, the number of roots modulo prime p is at most d (by Lagrange's theorem). This is used to analyze when a polynomial vanishes at many primes.\n   217\t\n   218\t**Key lemma:** If f(p) ≡ 0 (mod p) for all primes p in an infinite set, and f has degree d < p for large p, then either f ≡ 0 or p | leading coefficient for infinitely many p (both impossible if leading coefficient is fixed nonzero). Hence f ≡ 0.\n   219\t\n   220\t**Stronger result:** If D(r) = 2(-1)^{r+1} + r! is a fixed integer and D(r) ≡ 0 (mod p) for all primes p > r, then D(r) = 0 (since only finitely many primes divide a fixed nonzero integer).\n   221\t\n   222\t### 6.3 Divisibility Chains\n   223\t\n   224\tWhen establishing p | f(n) for a prime p and fixed polynomial f, a common technique is:\n   225\t1. Write f(n) in terms of (n-1)! using Wilson's theorem\n   226\t2. Compute (n-r)! in terms of (n-1)! using the factorial identity\n   227\t3. Derive a fixed constant that must be ≡ 0 (mod p) for all large primes p\n   228\t4. Conclude the constant is 0\n   229\t\n   230\t**Evaluation criterion:** Step 4 requires the explicit argument that a fixed nonzero integer cannot be divisible by all sufficiently large primes. This is often stated as \"the only way infinitely many primes can divide a fixed integer is if that integer is zero.\" This argument is valid and may be stated briefly in a competition proof.\n   231\t\n   232\t### 6.4 Parameterization of Divisors\n   233\t\n   234\tWhen p^k | f(a,b) and p ∤ a and p ∤ b, we can sometimes factor f and analyze which prime power divides each factor separately. This often involves writing f(a,b) = g(a,b) · h(a,b) and showing gcd(g,h) | m for some explicit m.\n   235\t\n   236\t---\n   237\t\n   238\t## Part VII: Standard Techniques Specific to the Proofs in This Audit\n   239\t\n   240\t### 7.1 Polynomial Divisibility and Wilson's Theorem (proof_01 type)\n   241\t\n   242\t**Problem type:** Find all monic polynomials f ∈ Z[x] such that p | 2(f(p))!+1 for all large primes p.\n   243\t\n   244\t**Standard approach:**\n   245\t1. **Degree bound:** For deg f ≥ 2, f(p) ≥ p for large p, so p | (f(p))! and thus 2(f(p))!+1 ≡ 1 (mod p) ≠ 0. Eliminates deg ≥ 2.\n   246\t2. **Constant polynomials:** f ≡ 1 (monic degree 0) gives 2·1!+1 = 3, only finitely many primes divide 3. Contradiction.\n   247\t3. **Linear case:** f(x) = x+b. Positive b gives f(p) ≥ p → contradiction (same as deg ≥ 2). So b < 0, write b = -r, r > 0.\n   248\t4. **Wilson application:** (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)···(p-r)·(p-r-1)! ≡ (-1)^r r! · (p-r-1)! (mod p). Combined with (p-1)! ≡ -1: get (p-r-1)! ≡ (-1)^{r+1}/(r!) (mod p).\n   249\t5. **Fixed integer condition:** 2(p-r-1)!+1 ≡ 0 (mod p) translates to the fixed integer D_r = 2(-1)^{r+1} + r! ≡ 0 (mod p) for all large p. Hence D_r = 0.\n   250\t6. **Find r:** Check small r until D_r = 0. Only r = 3 works: 2(-1)^4 + 3! = 2+6? No wait, let me recheck.\n   251\t\n   252\tActually for D_r = 2(-1)^{r+1} + r! (before the multiply through by r!), the condition after multiplying is: r! · 2(-1)^{r+1} + (r!)² = 0, or ... let me be careful. The exact form of D_r depends on the precise derivation. The proof should arrive at a specific integer that must be zero, and then check r=1,2,3,...\n   253\t\n   254\t**Correctness check:** A correct proof must:\n   255\t- Correctly derive the exact fixed integer D_r involving r\n   256\t- Note that this being ≡ 0 (mod p) for all large p forces D_r = 0\n   257\t- Correctly compute the specific value of r that makes D_r = 0\n   258\t- Verify that this r gives a working polynomial f(x) = x - r\n   259\t\n   260\t**First error in flawed proofs:** If the proof arrives at the wrong equation for D_r (perhaps confusing (p-r-1)! with (p-r)!), the final equation to solve will be wrong and the claimed value of r will be incorrect.\n   261\t\n   262\t### 7.2 Gaussian Integer Factorization Problems (proof_06 type)\n   263\t\n   264\t**Problem type:** Given a⁴+b⁴ = c⁴+d⁴ = e⁵ with distinct positive integers, show ac+bd is composite (or more generally, analyze the algebraic structure).\n   265\t\n   266\t**Standard approach:**\n   267\t1. **Reduction:** By coprimality of a,b,c,d,e (if a common factor p existed, divide through and repeat), assume gcd = 1.\n   268\t2. **Factorization in Z[i]:** a⁴+b⁴ = (a²+ib²)(a²-ib²). Show these factors are coprime in Z[i].\n   269\t3. **UFD consequence:** Each factor is a fifth power times a unit: a²+ib² = ε·α⁵ for some α = u+vi ∈ Z[i] and unit ε.\n   270\t4. **Norm equation:** N(a²+ib²) = a⁴+b⁴ = e⁵. N(α⁵) = N(α)⁵ = (u²+v²)⁵. Hence e = u²+v².\n   271\t5. **Repeat for (c,d):** c²+id² = ε'·α⁵ for some unit ε'. Since both pairs factorize in terms of the same α = u+vi...\n   272\t\n   273\t**Critical subtlety:** At step 5, the claim that (c²+id²) must involve the SAME α as (a²+ib²) requires justification. Both (a²+ib²) and (c²+id²) equal fifth powers (times units) of Gaussian integers with norm e = u²+v². But different choices of unit ε could give genuinely different fifth-power Gaussian integers. The argument \"the assignment must swap because the pairs are distinct\" needs to be made rigorous.\n   274\t\n   275\t**Evaluation criterion:** A proof that hand-waves the step \"hence the two factorizations must be related by swapping u+vi and u-vi\" without justifying why has a logical gap. Moreover, the conclusion c²=a², d²=-b² cannot hold for positive real a,b,c,d. If the proof DOES correctly derive a contradiction, the verdict is correct. If it makes an unjustified leap to derive this contradiction, the verdict is incorrect.\n   276\t\n   277\t### 7.3 Divisibility Congruences and Case Analysis (proof_10 type)\n   278\t\n   279\t**Problem type:** Prove or disprove that specific prime-power Diophantine equations have solutions.\n   280\t\n   281\t**Common structure:** Write the equation as p · f(q,b) = g(b) for primes p and q, then case-analyze on q = 2 vs q odd.\n   282\t\n   283\t**Evaluation criterion for case q=2:** The claim \"p | 4·7^b - 1 implies p = 3\" requires showing that 4·7^b ≡ 1 (mod p) has solutions only for p = 3. This requires either:\n   284\t(a) Computing ord_p(7) and checking divisibility conditions, OR\n   285\t(b) Direct case analysis for small primes\n   286\t\n   287\tSimply asserting the conclusion without either computation or case analysis is an unjustified gap.\n   288\t\n   289\t### 7.4 Algebraic Inequalities in Number Theory (proof_17 type)\n   290\t\n   291\t**Problem type:** Use algebraic inequalities to bound prime-related quantities.\n   292\t\n   293\t**Key principle:** When a proof uses an estimate like \"Kp^a < Mp^b\" for all positive p, both K and M must be explicitly computed, and the direction of the inequality must be verified for all relevant cases.\n   294\t\n   295\t**Common error:** Writing an inequality in the wrong direction (e.g., 27p² < 9p² is false for all positive p). This immediately invalidates any argument that depends on it.\n   296\t\n   297\t**Evaluation criterion:** Check every inequality claim. If any single inequality is in the wrong direction for the claimed values, the proof is incorrect at that step, regardless of whether the conclusion might be provable by other means.\n   298\t\n   299\t---\n   300\t\n   301\t## Part VIII: Evaluation Rubric Summary for Number Theory Proofs\n   302\t\n   303\tWhen evaluating a number theory proof in this audit, apply the following checklist:\n   304\t\n   305\t1. **Factual accuracy:** Are all theorems cited correctly? (Wilson, Fermat, Euler, LTE, QR)\n   306\t2. **Hypothesis completeness:** Are all hypotheses of each theorem explicitly verified before application?\n   307\t3. **Case completeness:** Are all cases covered? (Especially: what if q = 2? what if p = 2? what if the base case fails?)\n   308\t4. **Fixed-integer arguments:** When a fixed integer must be divisible by all large primes, is the conclusion \"hence the integer is 0\" correctly derived?\n   309\t5. **Coprimality:** When coprimality of two expressions is needed, is it proved or given?\n   310\t6. **UFD consequences:** When factoring in Z[i] or other number rings, is unique factorization correctly applied?\n   311\t7. **Direction of inequalities:** Are all stated inequalities in the correct direction for the claimed parameters?\n   312\t\n   313\tA proof with ANY false claim, unjustified transition, or missing critical case at any of these points should be marked **incorrect**, and the FIRST such occurrence is the first material issue.\n   314\t\n   315\t---\n   316\t\n   317\t## Part IX: Extended Examples of Correct and Incorrect Number Theory Reasoning\n   318\t\n   319\t### Example 1: Correct use of Wilson's Theorem\n   320\t\n   321\t**Claim:** For prime p > 5, p | (p-4)! + (p-3)·(p-4)!/p + ... \n   322\t\n   323\t**Approach:** Start from (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3)(p-4)! ≡ (-1)(-2)(-3)(p-4)! = -6(p-4)! (mod p). Since (p-1)! ≡ -1 (mod p), we get -6(p-4)! ≡ -1 (mod p), so (p-4)! ≡ (6)^{-1} ≡ (6)^{-1} (mod p). **This is a correct use of Wilson's theorem**, assuming the inverses exist (which they do for p > 3).\n   324\t\n   325\t### Example 2: Incorrect application — wrong formula\n   326\t\n   327\t**Claim:** (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)···(p-r) · (p-r)! (off-by-one error in factorial)\n   328\t\n   329\tThis is FALSE: (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)···(p-r) · (p-r-1)!, not (p-r)!. An off-by-one error in the factorial formula would give a completely wrong value for D_r, leading to an incorrect determination of r.\n   330\t\n   331\t### Example 3: Correct coprimality argument in Z[i]\n   332\t\n   333\t**Claim:** gcd_{Z[i]}(a²+ib², a²-ib²) is a unit, given that a⁴+b⁴ = e⁵ and gcd(a,b) = 1.\n   334\t\n   335\t**Proof:** Let δ = gcd(a²+ib², a²-ib²). Then δ | (a²+ib²)-(a²-ib²) = 2ib². Also δ | (a²+ib²)+(a²-ib²) = 2a². So δ | gcd(2ib², 2a²) = 2·gcd(b², a²)·i. Since gcd(a,b) = 1, gcd(a²,b²) = 1 in Z, so δ | 2i. The only Gaussian prime dividing 2i = -i(1+i)² is (1+i). If (1+i) | (a²+ib²), then N(1+i)=2 | N(a²+ib²) = a⁴+b⁴ = e⁵, so 2 | e⁵, hence 2 | e. Then 2^5 | e⁵ = a⁴+b⁴. But if a and b are both odd, a⁴+b⁴ ≡ 1+1 = 2 (mod 4), not 0 (mod 32). Careful analysis shows (1+i) ∤ (a²+ib²) under the coprimality assumptions. **This is a correct argument.**\n   336\t\n   337\t### Example 4: Incorrect — overlooked unit ambiguity\n   338\t\n   339\t**Claim:** Since a²+ib² = (u+vi)⁵ and c²+id² = ε'(u+vi)⁵ for unit ε', and (a²,b²) ≠ (c²,d²), the unit ε' must be i or -i, forcing c² = -a², d² = b².\n   340\t\n   341\t**Error:** This doesn't follow. The unit ε' could be 1, -1, i, or -i. The case ε' = -1 gives c²+id² = -(u+vi)⁵ = (-u-vi)⁵... one needs to trace through each case systematically. Simply asserting \"it must be i or -i\" based on distinctness is an unjustified leap.\n   342\t\n   343\t### Example 5: Correct evaluation — catch the wrong inequality\n   344\t\n   345\t**Claim in proof:** \"Since 3(k+1)p² ≤ 9p² for k ≥ 2, we have...\"\n   346\t\n   347\t**Check:** 3(k+1) ≤ 9 iff k+1 ≤ 3 iff k ≤ 2. For k ≥ 2, we have k+1 ≥ 3 so 3(k+1) ≥ 9. The inequality is BACKWARDS for k ≥ 3. This is the first material issue.\n   348\t\n   349\t### Example 6: Correct handling of cases in divisibility\n   350\t\n   351\t**Claim:** For the equation 4p^k = 7^m + 1, analyzing case p = 2: we get 2^{k+2} = 7^m + 1. Modulo 4: 0 ≡ 7^m + 1 ≡ 3^m + 1 (mod 4). For m even: 3^m ≡ 1 (mod 4), so 3^m+1 ≡ 2 (mod 4), but 2^{k+2} ≡ 0 (mod 4) for k ≥ 0. Contradiction unless k+2 = 1 i.e. k = -1 (not valid). For m odd: 3^m ≡ 3 (mod 4), so 3^m+1 ≡ 0 (mod 4). OK so m must be odd. Continue: 2^{k+2} = 7^m+1 and m odd... **This is a correct case analysis approach** — systematically checking mod small numbers to constrain the variables.\n   352\t\n   353\t---\n   354\t\n   355\t## Part X: Summary of First Material Issues Commonly Found\n   356\t\n   357\tIn number theory proofs at olympiad level, the most common first material issues are:\n   358\t\n   359\t1. **Wrong inequality direction** — claimed \"a < b\" when actually a ≥ b for the values in question\n   360\t2. **Missing case** — analyzed case p odd but not p = 2 (or vice versa), or analyzed cases modulo 3 but missed a residue class\n   361\t3. **Unjustified divisibility claim** — said \"p | N implies p = q\" without showing why\n   362\t4. **Incorrect application of Wilson** — used wrong factorial or wrong parity of (-1)\n   363\t5. **Coprimality assumed, not proven** — applied UFD factoring without establishing coprimality\n   364\t6. **Wrong final value** — computed D_r = 0 for wrong r, claiming incorrect polynomial\n   365\t7. **Overlooked units in Z[i]** — in Gaussian integer arguments, forgot to account for all four units {1, -1, i, -i}\n   366\t8. **Circular case elimination** — \"proved\" case q=2 by assuming p=3 and checking consistency, rather than deriving p=3 from q=2\n   367\t\n   368\tAny of the above at the FIRST occurrence in a proof constitutes a material issue and should be reported as the first_material_issue_summary in your audit.\n   369\t\n   370\t---\n   371\t\n   372\t## Appendix A: Extended Worked Examples — Number Theory Proof Evaluation\n   373\t\n   374\t### A.1 Wilson's Theorem Applications — 20 Evaluation Scenarios\n   375\t\n   376\t**Scenario 1 (Correct).** Claim: \"For prime p > 3, p divides (p-2)! - 1.\"\n   377\tProof: (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)! ≡ -1 (mod p). Also (p-1) ≡ -1 (mod p), so (-1)(p-2)! ≡ -1 (mod p), giving (p-2)! ≡ 1 (mod p). Hence p | (p-2)! - 1. Assessment: CORRECT. Uses Wilson properly, all steps valid.\n   378\t\n   379\t**Scenario 2 (Incorrect — wrong case boundary).** Claim: \"For prime p ≥ 2, p | (p-1)!+1.\"\n   380\tProof: By Wilson's theorem, (p-1)! ≡ -1 (mod p), so (p-1)!+1 ≡ 0 (mod p). Assessment: INCORRECT first material issue. For p = 2: (2-1)! = 1! = 1, and 1+1 = 2. 2|2 holds. But the proof works for all primes p ≥ 2. Actually CORRECT — Wilson holds for p=2: (2-1)! = 1 ≡ -1 ≡ 1 (mod 2), so (2-1)!+1 = 2 ≡ 0 (mod 2). So this proof is correct after all.\n   381\t\n   382\t**Scenario 3 (Incorrect — composite modulus).** Claim: \"15 | (15-1)! + 1 by Wilson's theorem.\"\n   383\tFirst material issue: Wilson's theorem requires p to be prime. 15 = 3×5 is not prime. In fact (14)! = 14·13·...·1 contains factors 3, 5, 6, 10, 15... wait, 15 ∤ {1,...,14}, but 3·5 = 15 ∤ any single element yet 3 and 5 are both in {1,...,14}. So 15 | 14! and 14!+1 ≡ 1 (mod 15) ≠ 0. INCORRECT: first issue is applying Wilson to non-prime 15.\n   384\t\n   385\t**Scenario 4 (Correct).** Claim: \"For prime p, ((p-1)/2)!² ≡ (-1)^{(p+1)/2} (mod p).\"\n   386\tProof: (p-1)! ≡ -1. Pair j with p-j for j=1,...,(p-1)/2. Product of each pair = j(p-j) ≡ j(-j) = -j² (mod p). Product of all pairs = Π_{j=1}^{(p-1)/2} (-j²) = (-1)^{(p-1)/2} · (((p-1)/2)!)². So (p-1)! = (-1)^{(p-1)/2} · (((p-1)/2)!)² ≡ -1. Hence (((p-1)/2)!)² ≡ (-1)^{(p+1)/2}. CORRECT.\n   387\t\n   388\t**Scenario 5 (Incorrect — off-by-one).** Claim: \"For prime p and r=3: (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3) · (p-3)!.\"\n   389\tFirst material issue: (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3)(p-4)···1. Extracting the first 3 terms: (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3) · (p-4)!, NOT (p-3)!. The off-by-one error gives the wrong factorial: (p-3)! vs (p-4)!.\n   390\t\n   391\t**Scenario 6 (Correct).** Claim: \"Let D₃ = 2(-1)^4 + 3! = 2 + 6 = 8. Since D₃ ≠ 0, r = 3 is not a solution.\"\n   392\tThe correct formula is D_r = 2(-1)^r + (r-1)! and one checks r=3 gives D_3 = -2+2=0.\n   393\t\n   394\t**Scenario W7 (Correct — Wilson derivation step).** \"(p-1)! = product_{j=1}^{p-1} j = (product_{j=1}^{r} (p-j)) * (product_{j=1}^{p-r-1} j) = (product_{j=1}^r (-j) (mod p)) * (p-r-1)! = (-1)^r*r!*(p-r-1)! (mod p).\" ✓\n   395\t\n   396\t**Scenario W8 (Correct — fixed integer argument).** \"The quantity 2(-1)^r + (r-1)! is a fixed integer independent of p. If this integer is nonzero, it has finitely many prime divisors, so only finitely many primes p can satisfy the congruence. Hence it must equal 0.\" ✓\n   397\t\n   398\t**Scenario W9 (Incorrect — r=2 claimed as solution).** Suppose someone computes D_2 = 2(-1)^2 + 1! = 2+1 = 3 ≠ 0, yet claims r=2 is a solution. INCORRECT: D_2 = 3 ≠ 0 means p | 3 for all large p, which fails for p > 3. Not a solution.\n   399\t\n   400\t**Scenario W10 (Correct — verification for r=3).** \"f(x)=x-3. For prime p > 3: (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3)*(p-4)!... wait, (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3)*(p-3-1)! hmm. Let me use: (p-1)(p-2)(p-3) ≡ (-1)(-2)(-3) = -6 (mod p). And (p-1)! = (-6)*(p-4)!. But (p-1)! ≡ -1. So (-6)*(p-4)! ≡ -1, giving (p-4)! ≡ 1/6 (mod p). Hmm, this doesn't directly give 2(p-3)!+1. Better: (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3)*(p-3-1)! hmm again. Let me think. Actually (p-3)! * (p-2) * (p-1) = (p-1)! but that's wrong too. (p-3)! * 1 = (p-3)! and we need to get from (p-3)! to (p-1)!. \n   401\t\n   402\t(p-1)! = (p-3)! * (p-2) * (p-1) ≡ (p-3)! * (-2)(-1) = 2*(p-3)! (mod p). So 2*(p-3)! ≡ (p-1)! ≡ -1 (mod p). Hence 2*(p-3)!+1 ≡ 0 (mod p). ✓\"\n   403\t\n   404\tThis is the cleanest verification for r=3. Note: the key step is (p-1)! = (p-3)! * (p-2) * (p-1) ≡ (p-3)! * (-2)(-1) = 2*(p-3)! (mod p). The formula is (p-r-1)! going from (p-1)! by stripping the r=3 terms: \n   405\t\n   406\tActually: f(p)! = (p-3)!. And (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3) * (p-4)!. \n   407\tHmm: p-3 terms from (p-1) to (p-3) are: (p-1)(p-2)(p-3). And (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3)(p-4)!.\n   408\tBut (p-3)! = (p-3)(p-4)! ... no, (p-3)! = 1*2*...*p-3 = (p-4)! * (p-3).\n   409\tSo (p-4)! = (p-3)! / (p-3).\n   410\t\n   411\t(p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3) * (p-4)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3) * (p-3)!/(p-3) = (p-1)(p-2) * (p-3)!.\n   412\t\n   413\tWait: (p-1)(p-2)(p-3) * (p-4)! = (p-1)! means (p-3)! = (p-3)(p-4)! (by definition). So (p-4)! = (p-3)!/(p-3). And (p-1)! = (p-1)(p-2)(p-3) * (p-3)!/(p-3) = (p-1)(p-2)*(p-3)!.\n   414\t\n   415\t(p-1)(p-2) ≡ (-1)(-2) = 2 (mod p). So (p-1)! ≡ 2*(p-3)! (mod p). By Wilson: -1 ≡ 2*(p-3)! (mod p). Hence 2*(p-3)! ≡ -1, so 2*(p-3)!+1 ≡ 0 (mod p). ✓\n   416\t\n   417\t### D.2 Gaussian Integer Arguments — 20 Additional Scenarios\n   418\t\n   419\t**GI1 (Correct — basic arithmetic in Z[i]).** \"(2+3i) * (1-i) = 2 - 2i + 3i - 3i^2 = 2 - 2i + 3i + 3 = 5 + i.\" N(2+3i) = 13. N(1-i) = 2. N(5+i) = 26 = 13*2. ✓\n   420\t\n   421\t**GI2 (Correct — Gaussian prime check).** \"Is 3 a Gaussian prime? 3 ≡ 3 (mod 4). Primes p ≡ 3 (mod 4) remain prime in Z[i]. So 3 is a Gaussian prime.\" ✓\n   422\t\n   423\t**GI3 (Correct — Gaussian prime factorization).** \"Factor 5 in Z[i]: 5 = (2+i)(2-i). N(2+i) = 5 prime in Z. 5 ≡ 1 (mod 4). So 5 splits.\" ✓\n   424\t\n   425\t**GI4 (Incorrect — confusing Z and Z[i] primes).** \"3 is prime in Z, so 3 is prime in Z[i].\"\n   426\tINCORRECT: Not all rational primes are Gaussian primes. Primes p ≡ 1 (mod 4) split in Z[i]. However, primes p ≡ 3 (mod 4) do remain prime. Since 3 ≡ 3 (mod 4), 3 IS a Gaussian prime. But the claim \"Z-prime implies Z[i]-prime\" is WRONG IN GENERAL (e.g., 5 = (2+i)(2-i) splits). The reasoning is wrong even though the conclusion for p=3 is correct.\n   427\t\n   428\t**GI5 (Correct — norm multiplicativity).** \"N(alpha * beta) = N(alpha) * N(beta) for all alpha, beta in Z[i].\" This follows from N(a+bi) = a^2+b^2 = |a+bi|^2 and |alpha*beta| = |alpha|*|beta| (complex modulus is multiplicative). ✓\n   429\t\n   430\t**GI6 (Correct — coprimality check).** \"gcd_{Z[i]}(a^2+ib^2, a^2-ib^2) with gcd(a,b)=1: any common divisor d divides (a^2+ib^2)-(a^2-ib^2) = 2ib^2 and (a^2+ib^2)+(a^2-ib^2) = 2a^2. So d | gcd_{Z[i]}(2ib^2, 2a^2). Since 2i is a unit times (1+i)^2 and gcd(a,b)=1 gives gcd(a^2,b^2)=1: the only common Gaussian prime could be (1+i). Analysis of whether (1+i) can divide both factors requires checking if 2 | a^4+b^4. If a and b are both odd: a^4+b^4 ≡ 1+1 = 2 (mod 4), so v_2(a^4+b^4) = 1, not divisible by 4, so (1+i) divides a^2+ib^2 to a limited power. The full coprimality analysis is standard.\" ✓ (Correct approach, details omitted)\n   431\t\n   432\t**GI7 (Incorrect — wrong unit count).** \"The units of Z[i] form a group {1,-1} under multiplication.\"\n   433\tINCORRECT. The units of Z[i] are {1,-1,i,-i}. The group is cyclic of order 4 generated by i. Omitting ±i is a first material issue in any proof that then says \"the only units are ±1.\"\n   434\t\n   435\t**GI8 (Correct — fifth power structure).** \"If alpha = u+vi with N(alpha)^5 = N(e^5) = e^5, then N(alpha) = e (taking the unique positive real 5th root). So (u+vi)^5 = epsilon*(a^2+ib^2) for some unit epsilon in {1,-1,i,-i}.\"\n   436\tCORRECT. N((u+vi)^5) = N(u+vi)^5 = (u^2+v^2)^5. Setting = N(a^2+ib^2) = a^4+b^4 = e^5 gives (u^2+v^2)^5 = e^5, so u^2+v^2 = e. ✓\n   437\t\n   438\t**GI9 (Incorrect — missing the swap case).** \"Since (c^2+id^2) = epsilon'*(u+vi)^5 with epsilon' a unit and (a^2,b^2) != (c^2,d^2), the unit epsilon' must give a DIFFERENT expression than (a^2+ib^2) = epsilon*(u+vi)^5. The only other fifth power is (u-vi)^5 (complex conjugate). So epsilon'*(u+vi)^5 = (some unit)*(u-vi)^5, giving c^2+id^2 is related to u-vi.\"\n   439\t\n   440\tPARTIALLY CORRECT: (c^2+id^2) and (a^2+ib^2) are indeed fifth powers (times units) of Gaussian integers of norm e. But there are more than just (u+vi)^5 and (u-vi)^5 up to units. Specifically: {(u+vi)^5, i*(u+vi)^5, -1*(u+vi)^5, -i*(u+vi)^5, (u-vi)^5, i*(u-vi)^5, -1*(u-vi)^5, -i*(u-vi)^5} are all fifth powers of Gaussian integers of norm e. The proof must carefully enumerate which unit makes the real and imaginary parts equal to c^2 and d^2 (which must be non-negative since c,d are positive integers).\n   441\t\n   442\t**GI10 (Correct — real and imaginary part constraints).** \"From c^2+id^2 = epsilon'*(u+vi)^5 with c,d > 0:\n   443\tIf epsilon'=1: Re = Re((u+vi)^5) = u^5-10u^3v^2+5uv^4 = u(u^4-10u^2v^2+5v^4). Must be c^2 > 0.\n   444\tIf epsilon'=-1: Re = -(u^5-10u^3v^2+5uv^4) = -c^2 < 0. IMPOSSIBLE for c^2 > 0.\n   445\tIf epsilon'=i: Re = -Im((u+vi)^5) = -(5u^4v-10u^2v^3+v^5) = -v(5u^4-10u^2v^2+v^4). Must be c^2 > 0. Requires v(5u^4-10u^2v^2+v^4) < 0.\n   446\tIf epsilon'=-i: Re = Im((u+vi)^5) = v(5u^4-10u^2v^2+v^4) = c^2 > 0. Requires this to be positive.\" ✓\n   447\t\n   448\tThis analysis is CORRECT and shows that the unit epsilon' is constrained by sign conditions on c,d,u,v. The claim \"it must swap u+vi and u-vi\" is only valid after this sign analysis.\n   449\t\n   450\t### D.3 Prime Factorization and Divisibility — 20 Scenarios\n   451\t\n   452\t**P1 (Correct).** \"v_2(2^k) = k.\" TRIVIALLY CORRECT.\n   453\t\n   454\t**P2 (Correct).** \"v_p(p^a * q^b) = a for prime p != q.\" CORRECT by unique factorization.\n   455\t\n   456\t**P3 (Correct).** \"v_p(a+b) >= min(v_p(a), v_p(b)) with equality if v_p(a) != v_p(b).\" CORRECT (ultrametric property).\n   457\t\n   458\t**P4 (Incorrect).** \"v_p(a+b) = min(v_p(a), v_p(b)) always.\"\n   459\tINCORRECT: If v_p(a) = v_p(b), equality need not hold. E.g., a=p, b=-p: a+b=0, v_p(0)=infinity > min(1,1)=1.\n   460\tOr: a=p, b=p: a+b=2p, v_p(2p)=1=min(1,1) IF p!=2. But a=2, b=2: a+b=4=2^2, v_2(4)=2 > min(v_2(2),v_2(2))=min(1,1)=1.\n   461\t\n   462\t**P5 (Correct — Legendre's formula).** \"v_p(n!) = sum_{k=1}^infinity floor(n/p^k) = (n - s_p(n))/(p-1) where s_p(n) = digit sum in base p.\"\n   463\tCORRECT. Both formulas are valid. The sum formula is direct (count multiples of p, p^2, etc.). The closed form follows from the base-p expansion.\n   464\t\n   465\t**P6 (Correct — application).** \"v_5(100!) = floor(100/5)+floor(100/25)+floor(100/125)+... = 20+4+0 = 24.\"\n   466\tCORRECT. ✓\n   467\t\n   468\t**P7 (Incorrect — off-by-one in Legendre).** \"v_2(10!) = floor(10/2)+floor(10/4)+floor(10/8) = 5+2+1+0 = 8.\"\n   469\tCORRECT (checking): 8 = v_2(10!) since 10! = 3628800 = 2^8 * (odd). Indeed: 2,4,6,8,10 contribute 1,2,1,3,1 factors of 2: 1+2+1+3+1=8. ✓ So this is CORRECT.\n   470\t\n   471\t**P8 (Incorrect — wrong lower bound).** \"For a,b > 0 with a+b = n: v_p(a) + v_p(b) <= v_p(n) + v_p(C(n,a)).\"\n   472\tActually this is v_p(C(n,a)) = v_p(n!) - v_p(a!) - v_p(b!). This is the Kummer-like formula. The inequality v_p(a)+v_p(b) <= v_p(n) + v_p(C(n,a)) rearranges to v_p(C(n,a)) >= v_p(a)+v_p(b)-v_p(n)... checking this for specific cases could verify or falsify.\n   473\t\n   474\t**P9 (Correct — prime detection via factorial).** \"If n = p is prime: v_p((p-1)!) = 0 (since all factors are 1,...,p-1, none divisible by p). And v_p(p!) = 1.\" ✓\n   475\t\n   476\t**P10 (Correct — Zsygmondy application).** \"For a>b>0, gcd(a,b)=1, n>=3: a^n-b^n has a primitive prime divisor q (a prime q divides a^n-b^n but does not divide a^k-b^k for k<n), with exceptions: (a,b,n)=(2,1,6).\"\n   477\tCORRECT (Zsygmondy's theorem). This is often used in olympiad number theory to show expressions have large prime factors.\n   478\t\n   479\t### D.4 Modular Arithmetic Reference — 15 Scenarios\n   480\t\n   481\t**M1 (Correct).** \"7^4 ≡ 1 (mod 5). Since 4 = phi(5), this follows from Fermat.\" ✓\n   482\t\n   483\t**M2 (Incorrect).** \"7^5 ≡ 7 (mod 5) by Fermat's little theorem.\"\n   484\tINCORRECT: Fermat says a^{p-1} ≡ 1 (mod p), so 7^4 ≡ 1 (mod 5). Then 7^5 ≡ 7 (mod 5). And 7 ≡ 2 (mod 5). So 7^5 ≡ 2 (mod 5). The claim \"7^5 ≡ 7 (mod 5)\" is CORRECT in the sense that a^p ≡ a (mod p) by Fermat (multiplying 7^{p-1} ≡ 1 by 7). And 7 ≡ 2 (mod 5), so 7^5 ≡ 7 ≡ 2 (mod 5). The claim \"7^5 ≡ 7 (mod 5)\" is true but might be seen as not reduced.\n   485\t\n   486\t**M3 (Correct).** \"The order of 3 modulo 7 divides phi(7) = 6. So 3 is a primitive root mod 7.\" ✓\n   487\t\n   488\t**M4 (Correct).** \"For prime p ≡ 1 (mod 4), (-1/p) = 1 (i.e., -1 is a QR mod p).\"\n   489\tBy Euler's criterion: (-1/p) ≡ (-1)^{(p-1)/2} (mod p). Since p ≡ 1 (mod 4): (p-1)/2 is even, so (-1)^{(p-1)/2} = 1. ✓\n   490\t\n   491\t**M5 (Incorrect).** \"For prime p ≡ 3 (mod 4), -1 is a QR mod p.\"\n   492\tINCORRECT: For p ≡ 3 (mod 4), (p-1)/2 is odd, so (-1)^{(p-1)/2} = -1, meaning (-1/p) = -1, i.e., -1 is a NON-residue mod p. The claim is FALSE.\n   493\t\n   494\t**M6 (Correct).** \"Chinese Remainder Theorem: x ≡ 2 (mod 3), x ≡ 3 (mod 5). Solution: x ≡ 8 (mod 15). Sub-agents auditing **proof_02**, **proof_04**, **proof_09**, and **proof_16** should read this document before evaluating their assigned proof.\n     4\t\n     5\t---\n     6\t\n     7\t## Part I: Euclidean Geometry Fundamentals\n     8\t\n     9\t### 1.1 Angles and Circles\n    10\t\n    11\t**Inscribed angle theorem:** An angle inscribed in a circle is half the central angle that subtends the same arc. If A, B, C lie on a circle with center O and arc BC (not containing A), then ∠BAC = (1/2)·∠BOC.\n    12\t\n    13\t**Consequence:** Angles inscribed in the same arc are equal. If A, A' both lie on the same arc BC (i.e., same side), then ∠BAC = ∠BA'C.\n    14\t\n    15\t**Tangent-chord angle:** The angle between a tangent to a circle at point P and a chord PQ equals the inscribed angle in the alternate segment.\n    16\t\n    17\t**Power of a point:** For a point P and circle ω, the power of P is |PO|² - r² (where O is center, r is radius). If a line through P meets ω at X and Y, then PX · PY = power of P (with sign conventions for interior/exterior). For two chords through P intersecting circle: PA · PB = PC · PD.\n    18\t\n    19\t### 1.2 Cyclic Quadrilaterals\n    20\t\n    21\tFour points A, B, C, D are concyclic if and only if ∠ABC + ∠ADC = 180° (opposite angles of cyclic quadrilateral are supplementary). Equivalently, ∠ABD = ∠ACD (angles subtending the same arc AD from the same side).\n    22\t\n    23\t**Ptolemy's theorem:** For cyclic quadrilateral ABCD: AC · BD = AB · CD + AD · BC.\n    24\t\n    25\t### 1.3 Directed Angles Modulo 180°\n    26\t\n    27\tIn olympiad geometry, **directed angles** (angles mod 180°) are often more convenient than signed angles because they avoid case distinctions based on the configuration.\n    28\t\n    29\t**Definition:** ∠(ℓ, m) denotes the angle from line ℓ to line m, measured mod 180°. Equivalently, for points A, B, C: ∠ABC denotes the directed angle from ray BA to ray BC, mod 180°.\n    30\t\n    31\t**Key property:** A, B, C, D are concyclic if and only if ∠ACB = ∠ADB (directed angles mod 180°). This eliminates the need to specify which arc or which side.\n    32\t\n    33\t**Angle chasing rules:**\n    34\t- ∠(AB, CD) = -∠(CD, AB)\n    35\t- ∠(AB, CD) + ∠(CD, EF) = ∠(AB, EF)\n    36\t- If O is the center: ∠AOB = 2∠ACB (directed angles, where C is on the major arc)\n    37\t\n    38\t**Common error in directed angle proofs:** Failing to consistently use directed angles — mixing signed and unsigned angles within the same argument. If a step uses ∠ACB = 90° and another uses ∠ACB = -90° without acknowledging they represent the same directed angle, the proof is internally inconsistent.\n    39\t\n    40\t**Evaluation criterion:** In an angle-chase proof, every angle equality claimed between inscribed angles must follow from concyclicity of four points. If the claim \"∠PAQ = ∠PBQ\" is made, verify that P, A, B, Q are concyclic (or that PQ is a diameter and angles are right angles, etc.). An unjustified angle equality is a first material issue.\n    41\t\n    42\t### 1.4 Radical Axis and Coaxial Circles\n    43\t\n    44\tThe **radical axis** of two circles ω₁ and ω₂ is the locus of points with equal power with respect to both circles. It is always a line perpendicular to the line through the centers.\n    45\t\n    46\t**Radical center:** For three circles with pairwise non-parallel radical axes, the three radical axes meet at a single point, the radical center.\n    47\t\n    48\t**Coaxial circles:** A family of circles sharing a common radical axis. Any two circles in the family have the same radical axis.\n    49\t\n    50\t### 1.5 Inversion\n    51\t\n    52\t**Inversion with center O and radius r:** Maps each point P ≠ O to P' on ray OP with OP · OP' = r².\n    53\t\n    54\tKey properties:\n    55\t- Circles through O ↔ Lines not through O\n    56\t- Circles not through O ↔ Circles not through O\n    57\t- Lines through O ↔ Lines through O\n    58\t- Angles are preserved (inversion is conformal)\n    59\t- If P, Q, R, S are concyclic and O is not on their circle, their images P', Q', R', S' are also concyclic (or collinear if O is on the circle)\n    60\t\n    61\t---\n    62\t\n    63\t## Part II: Coordinate Geometry and Analytic Methods\n    64\t\n    65\t### 2.1 Setting Up Coordinates\n    66\t\n    67\t**Choice of coordinates:** For an isosceles triangle with AB = AC and ∠A = α, convenient choices include:\n    68\t- A at origin, axis of symmetry along y-axis: A=(0,0), B=(-b, 0), C=(b,0) with apex at (0,h)\n    69\t- A at origin with one leg along x-axis: A=(0,0), B=(1,0), C=(cos α, sin α)\n    70\t\n    71\tThe second choice places angle A at the origin explicitly and is often more natural.\n    72\t\n    73\t**Circumcenter computation:** The circumcenter O of triangle ABC is equidistant from A, B, C. For A=(0,0), B=(1,0), C=(cos θ, sin θ) with angle θ = 45°:\n    74\t- Perpendicular bisector of AB: x = 1/2\n    75\t- Perpendicular bisector of AC: midpoint of AC is (cos θ/2, sin θ/2), slope of AC is sin θ/cos θ = tan θ, so perpendicular bisector has slope -cot θ and passes through (cos θ/2, sin θ/2)\n    76\t\n    77\tFor θ = 45°: C = (√2/2, √2/2), midpoint = (√2/4, √2/4), perpendicular bisector has slope -1 and equation y - √2/4 = -(x - √2/4), i.e., y = -x + √2/2. Intersecting with x = 1/2: y = √2/2 - 1/2 = (√2-1)/2.\n    78\t\n    79\tHence O = (1/2, (√2-1)/2). Verify: |OA|² = 1/4 + (√2-1)²/4 = (1 + 3-2√2)/4 = (4-2√2)/4 = (2-√2)/2. And |OC|² = (√2/2 - 1/2)² + (√2/2 - (√2-1)/2)² = ((√2-1)/2)² + (1/2)² = (3-2√2+1)/4 = (4-2√2)/4. ✓\n    80\t\n    81\t### 2.2 Finding Circle Intersections\n    82\t\n    83\tTo find where two circles intersect: subtract one equation from the other to get a linear equation (the radical axis), then substitute back.\n    84\t\n    85\t**Example:** Circle 1: (x-h₁)²+(y-k₁)²=r₁² and Circle 2: (x-h₂)²+(y-k₂)²=r₂². Subtracting: 2(h₂-h₁)x + 2(k₂-k₁)y = r₁²-r₂²+h₂²-h₁²+k₂²-k₁². This is the radical axis — a line.\n    86\t\n    87\t**Checking collinearity:** Three points P₁, P₂, P₃ are collinear if and only if the vectors P₁P₂ and P₁P₃ are parallel, i.e., the determinant |x₁-x₃, y₁-y₃; x₂-x₃, y₂-y₃| = 0. Equivalently, slopes of P₁P₂ and P₁P₃ are equal.\n    88\t\n    89\t**Checking concurrence:** Three lines L₁, L₂, L₃ are concurrent if and only if any two of the three pairwise intersection points coincide. In coordinate proofs, one typically finds the intersection P = L₁∩L₂ and then verifies P ∈ L₃.\n    90\t\n    91\t### 2.3 Line Slope Computations\n    92\t\n    93\tFor points P = (x₁, y₁) and Q = (x₂, y₂) with x₁ ≠ x₂:\n    94\tslope(PQ) = (y₂-y₁)/(x₂-x₁)\n    95\t\n    96\t**Rationalization:** When slopes involve √2, rationalize: 1/(√2-1) = (√2+1)/((√2-1)(√2+1)) = (√2+1)/(2-1) = √2+1.\n    97\t\n    98\t**Perpendicularity:** Lines with slopes m and m' are perpendicular iff mm' = -1. Lines with the same slope are parallel.\n    99\t\n   100\t**Evaluation criterion for coordinate proofs:** Every arithmetic step (especially with surds like √2) must be correct. A computation error in the coordinates of O, F, E, Z, or P invalidates all subsequent claims that depend on those coordinates. Verify key steps: circumradius formula, circle-intersection radical axis formula, substitution into line equation.\n   101\t\n   102\t### 2.4 The Circle-Chord Characterization\n   103\t\n   104\tFor a circle ω with center C and radius r, and a line ℓ, the chord of intersection can be found by:\n   105\t1. Computing the distance from C to ℓ\n   106\t2. Using the Pythagorean theorem to find half the chord length\n   107\t\n   108\tFor ℓ: ax+by=c, the distance from (h,k) to ℓ is |ah+bk-c|/√(a²+b²).\n   109\t\n   110\t---\n   111\t\n   112\t## Part III: Transformational Geometry\n   113\t\n   114\t### 3.1 Rotations\n   115\t\n   116\tA rotation by angle θ about center O transforms point P to point P' where:\n   117\t- |OP'| = |OP|\n   118\t- ∠POP' = θ (with appropriate orientation convention)\n   119\t\n   120\tIn Cartesian coordinates with center O = (a,b):\n   121\tx' - a = (x-a)cos θ - (y-b)sin θ\n   122\ty' - b = (x-a)sin θ + (y-b)cos θ\n   123\t\n   124\t**Special cases:**\n   125\t- θ = π (rotation by 180°): P' = 2O - P (point reflection through O)\n   126\t- θ = π/2: x'-a = -(y-b), y'-b = x-a (90° counterclockwise)\n   127\t- θ = -π/2: x'-a = y-b, y'-b = -(x-a) (90° clockwise)\n   128\t\n   129\t**Key property:** Rotation preserves distances and angles. If P maps to P' and Q maps to Q', then |P'Q'| = |PQ| and ∠P'O Q' = ∠POQ.\n   130\t\n   131\t### 3.2 Common Error: Wrong Rotation Angle\n   132\t\n   133\tIn olympiad problems involving rotations, the rotation angle must be derived from the geometric constraints, not assumed. Common sources of the rotation angle:\n   134\t- ∠BOC = 2∠BAC for inscribed angles (central angle = twice inscribed angle)\n   135\t- The angle between two chords meeting at a point\n   136\t- The supplement of an angle in a polygon\n   137\t\n   138\t**Evaluation criterion:** If a proof sets up a rotation with angle θ, verify that θ is correctly computed from the given data. An error in θ will cause all subsequent rotation-image relations to be wrong, even if the rotation technique is otherwise correctly applied.\n   139\t\n   140\t### 3.3 Reflections and Symmetry\n   141\t\n   142\tA reflection over line ℓ maps each point P to P' such that ℓ is the perpendicular bisector of PP'.\n   143\t\n   144\tFor ℓ: y = mx + b:\n   145\tIf m = 0 (horizontal): P' = (x, 2b-y)\n   146\tIf ℓ is the x-axis: P' = (x, -y)\n   147\tIf ℓ is the y-axis: P' = (-x, y)\n   148\tGeneral formula: if ℓ is y = mx and we reflect P = (p,q), then P' has coordinates determined by the projection formula.\n   149\t\n   150\t### 3.4 Homothety (Dilation)\n   151\t\n   152\tA homothety with center O and ratio k maps P to P' on ray OP with OP' = k·OP. For k < 0, P' is on the opposite ray.\n   153\t\n   154\t**Property:** Homothety maps circles to circles (same or opposite orientation depending on sign of k). The ratio of radii equals |k|.\n   155\t\n   156\t---\n   157\t\n   158\t## Part IV: Concurrence and Collinearity Theorems\n   159\t\n   160\t### 4.1 Ceva's Theorem\n   161\t\n   162\tIn triangle ABC, let D, E, F be points on sides BC, CA, AB respectively (or their extensions). The lines AD, BE, CF are concurrent (or all parallel) if and only if:\n   163\t\n   164\t(BD/DC) · (CE/EA) · (AF/FB) = 1\n   165\t\n   166\t(with signed ratios if points are on extensions).\n   167\t\n   168\t**Trigonometric form:** AD, BE, CF concur iff (sin∠BAD/sin∠DAC) · (sin∠CBE/sin∠EBA) · (sin∠ACF/sin∠FCB) = 1.\n   169\t\n   170\t### 4.2 Menelaus's Theorem\n   171\t\n   172\tPoints D on BC, E on CA, F on AB (or extensions) are collinear iff:\n   173\t(BD/DC) · (CE/EA) · (AF/FB) = -1\n   174\t\n   175\t(with signed ratios).\n   176\t\n   177\t### 4.3 Pascal's Theorem\n   178\t\n   179\tIf six points A, B, C, D, E, F lie on a conic (circle, ellipse, parabola, hyperbola), then the three intersection points of opposite sides of hexagon ABCDEF are collinear.\n   180\t\n   181\t**For circles:** This is a powerful tool for concurrence and collinearity in problems involving multiple points on a circle.\n   182\t\n   183\t### 4.4 Radical Axis Method for Concurrence\n   184\t\n   185\tThree circles are coaxial (share a common radical axis) if and only if their three pairwise radical axes are the same line. Three circles with different centers have a unique radical center.\n   186\t\n   187\tThis is sometimes used to prove that three lines (which happen to be radical axes of three circles) are concurrent.\n   188\t\n   189\t---\n   190\t\n   191\t## Part V: Geometry of Specific Configurations\n   192\t\n   193\t### 5.1 Isosceles Triangle with ∠A = 45°\n   194\t\n   195\tFor isosceles triangle ABC with AB = AC = 1 and ∠A = 45°:\n   196\t- ∠B = ∠C = (180°-45°)/2 = 67.5°\n   197\t- BC = 2sin(22.5°) ≈ 2 · 0.3827 ≈ 0.765 (using the law of sines or half-angle formula)\n   198\t\n   199\tIn coordinates (A at origin, B=(1,0), C=(cos45°, sin45°) = (√2/2, √2/2)):\n   200\t- Circumradius R = BC/(2sin∠A) = BC/(2sin45°) = BC/√2\n   201\t- Alternatively: R = |OA| = √{1/4 + (√2-1)²/4} = √{(2-√2)/2}\n   202\t\n   203\t### 5.2 Construction with Two Circles: Finding Intersections\n   204\t\n   205\tWhen two circles overlap, their intersection is characterized by the radical axis (linear equation). The actual intersection points satisfy both:\n   206\t1. The linear equation (from subtracting the two circle equations)\n   207\t2. Either circle's equation (substituting the linear relation back)\n   208\t\n   209\tThis always gives a quadratic with (generically) two solutions. To identify which intersection is which (e.g., \"Z on arc BC\" vs \"E on arc AC\"), substitute the candidate points into inequalities that characterize the arcs.\n   210\t\n   211\t### 5.3 The Foot of the Perpendicular\n   212\t\n   213\tThe foot D of the perpendicular from point C = (cx, cy) to line AB:\n   214\t- If AB is the x-axis: D = (cx, 0)\n   215\t- If AB has equation ax+by+c=0: D = C - ((a·cx+b·cy+c)/(a²+b²))·(a,b)\n   216\t\n   217\tThe distance CD = |a·cx+b·cy+c|/√(a²+b²).\n   218\t\n   219\t### 5.4 Line FM and Point P in Concurrence Proofs\n   220\t\n   221\tFor concurrence of three lines L₁, L₂, L₃:\n   222\t**Standard approach in coordinates:** Find P = L₁∩L₂ by solving the system of two linear equations. Verify P ∈ L₃ by substituting P's coordinates into L₃'s equation.\n   223\t\n   224\t**Slope method for collinearity:** For collinearity of points F, M, P, compute slope(FP) and slope(FM) separately (or FM and MP). They must be equal.\n   225\t\n   226\t---\n   227\t\n   228\t## Part VI: Combinatorial Geometry\n   229\t\n   230\t### 6.1 Configurations with Points on Circles\n   231\t\n   232\tWhen points are defined on circles (e.g., A_k defined as the k-th point in some sequence), claims that a third point lies on a specific circle must be justified by one of:\n   233\t- The circle's defining equation being satisfied\n   234\t- The inscribed angle theorem (finding equal inscribed angles subtending the same arc)\n   235\t- The power of the point condition being met\n   236\t- An explicit construction that places the point on the circle\n   237\t\n   238\t**Support point arguments:** In circle packing and convex hull problems, a \"support point\" is a point on the boundary of the convex hull. If a construction says \"A_k lies on circle ω,\" the justification must be either geometric (from angle conditions) or algebraic (coordinates satisfy the circle equation), not just asserted.\n   239\t\n   240\t**Evaluation criterion:** If a proof claims \"A_k lies on circle ω\" without justification — neither a geometric argument (angle equality, power of point) nor an algebraic check — this is a material gap. The entire subsequent argument that uses A_k ∈ ω fails if this claim is unverified.\n   241\t\n   242\t### 6.2 Convex Hulls and Support Lines\n   243\t\n   244\tA convex polygon's boundary consists of support lines: each edge ℓ of the polygon has all polygon vertices on one side (or on ℓ). A point Q is a vertex of the convex hull if and only if no line through Q has all other points strictly on one side.\n   245\t\n   246\t**In circle arguments:** If a circle ω is internally tangent to a circle Ω, and A_k is defined as the tangent point or a point on ω, saying A_k ∈ Ω requires showing A_k simultaneously lies on ω (by construction) and on Ω (by a separate argument). These are two distinct conditions.\n   247\t\n   248\t### 6.3 Intersection and Overlap of Circles\n   249\t\n   250\tTwo distinct circles intersect in 0, 1, or 2 points depending on the relationship between the distance d between centers and the radii r₁, r₂:\n   251\t- d > r₁+r₂: external, 0 intersections\n   252\t- d = r₁+r₂: externally tangent, 1 intersection\n   253\t- |r₁-r₂| < d < r₁+r₂: overlapping, 2 intersections\n   254\t- d = |r₁-r₂|: internally tangent, 1 intersection\n   255\t- d < |r₁-r₂|: one inside the other, 0 intersections\n   256\t\n   257\t---\n   258\t\n   259\t## Part VII: Standard Proof Evaluation Criteria for Geometry\n   260\t\n   261\t### 7.1 Angle-Chase Proofs\n   262\t\n   263\tAn angle-chase proof establishes a chain of angle equalities, each justified by one of:\n   264\t- Inscribed angle theorem (angles subtending same arc are equal)\n   265\t- Tangent-chord angle theorem\n   266\t- Vertical angles, supplementary angles, etc.\n   267\t- Parallel line angle relationships (alternate interior, corresponding, co-interior)\n   268\t\n   269\t**Evaluation criterion:** For EACH angle equality claimed, identify which theorem or rule justifies it. If a claim \"∠XYZ = ∠XWZ\" is made without specifying that X,Y,Z,W are concyclic (or another justification), it is unjustified. The first unjustified angle equality is the first material issue.\n   270\t\n   271\t**Directed angle advantage:** Using directed angles mod 180° eliminates the need to distinguish cases (which arc, which side). But it requires every angle equality to be valid as a directed angle equality. If a proof mixes directed and undirected angles, errors can arise.\n   272\t\n   273\t### 7.2 Coordinate Geometry Proofs\n   274\t\n   275\tA coordinate proof is valid if and only if:\n   276\t1. The coordinate setup correctly represents the given geometric configuration\n   277\t2. Every arithmetic computation is correct\n   278\t3. The final claim (concurrence, collinearity, etc.) is verified by the stated equalities/checks\n   279\t\n   280\t**Verification steps:**\n   281\t- Circumcenter: check it is equidistant from all three vertices\n   282\t- Intersection points: check they lie on both curves\n   283\t- Concurrence: check the intersection of two lines lies on the third\n   284\t- Collinearity: check equal slopes or zero determinant\n   285\t\n   286\t**Common arithmetic errors with √2:**\n   287\t- (√2-1)/2: correct, approximately 0.207\n   288\t- (√2+1)/2: correct, approximately 1.207\n   289\t- 1/(√2-1) = √2+1 after rationalization\n   290\t- (√2-1)² = 2-2√2+1 = 3-2√2\n   291\t- (√2/2)² = 1/2, not 2\n   292\t\n   293\t### 7.3 Rotation Proofs\n   294\t\n   295\t**Evaluation criterion:** A rotation proof must:\n   296\t1. Specify the center, angle, and orientation of the rotation\n   297\t2. Correctly compute the angle from the given data\n   298\t3. Correctly identify the image of each relevant point\n   299\t4. Verify that the image satisfies the claimed property\n   300\t\n   301\tThe rotation angle is typically derived from the angles of the figure. An error in computing the rotation angle (e.g., π instead of the correct angle) will cause the images to be incorrectly identified, invalidating all subsequent claims.\n   302\t\n   303\t**Warning:** A rotation by angle π is point reflection. This is only valid if the geometric configuration indeed has a 180° rotational symmetry, which must be established from the hypotheses, not assumed.\n   304\t\n   305\t---\n   306\t\n   307\t## Part VIII: Extended Examples for Geometry Proof Evaluation\n   308\t\n   309\t### Example 1: Valid angle-chase\n   310\t\n   311\t**Claim:** Let ABCD be a cyclic quadrilateral. Then ∠BAC = ∠BDC (since both are inscribed in circle and subtend arc BC from the same side).\n   312\t\n   313\t**Analysis:** This is CORRECT. Both A and D lie on the same arc (opposite arc BC), so the inscribed angles subtending BC from that arc are equal. ✓\n   314\t\n   315\t### Example 2: Invalid angle-chase — wrong concyclicity\n   316\t\n   317\t**Claim:** Let P, Q, R, S be four points with ∠PQR = ∠PSR = 50°. Then P, Q, R, S are concyclic.\n   318\t\n   319\t**Analysis:** This is BACKWARD. The converse is true: if P,Q,R,S are concyclic, then ∠PQR = ∠PSR. But the claim uses equal angles to conclude concyclicity — this IS valid by the converse of the inscribed angle theorem. So this claim is CORRECT. (Note: The converse holds as long as Q and S are on the same side of PR.)\n   320\t\n   321\t### Example 3: Unjustified claim in coordinate proof\n   322\t\n   323\t**Claim:** By direct computation, the circumcircle has equation (x-1/2)²+(y-k)²=R² for appropriate k.\n   324\t\n   325\t**Missing step:** The value of k must be computed, not just asserted to be \"appropriate.\" Without the explicit formula for k (derived from equating distances to the three vertices), the claim is unsupported.\n   326\t\n   327\t### Example 4: Slope computation with surds\n   328\t\n   329\t**Verify:** slope(CO) where C = (√2/2, √2/2) and O = (1/2, (√2-1)/2).\n   330\t\n   331\tslope = [(√2-1)/2 - √2/2] / [1/2 - √2/2]\n   332\t     = [(√2-1-√2)/2] / [(1-√2)/2]\n   333\t     = [-1/2] / [(1-√2)/2]\n   334\t     = -1/(1-√2)\n   335\t     = 1/(√2-1)\n   336\t     = (√2+1)/((√2-1)(√2+1))\n   337\t     = (√2+1)/(2-1)\n   338\t     = √2+1 ✓\n   339\t\n   340\tThis is a correct computation. The step 1/(√2-1) = √2+1 requires the rationalization step.\n   341\t\n   342\t### Example 5: Checking concurrence in coordinate proof\n   343\t\n   344\t**Method:** Lines L₁: y = (√2+1)x - 1 and L₂: y = 1/2-(√2-1)x.\n   345\t\n   346\tIntersection: (√2+1)x-1 = 1/2-(√2-1)x\n   347\t(√2+1+√2-1)x = 3/2\n   348\t2√2·x = 3/2\n   349\tx = 3/(4√2) = 3√2/8\n   350\t\n   351\ty = (√2+1)·3√2/8 - 1 = (3·2+3√2)/8 - 1 = (6+3√2-8)/8 = (3√2-2)/8\n   352\t\n   353\tPoint P = (3√2/8, (3√2-2)/8).\n   354\t\n   355\t**Checking P on line FM:** F = ((√2-1)/2, (√2-1)/2) and M = ((2+√2)/4, √2/4).\n   356\t\n   357\tslope(FM) = [√2/4 - (√2-1)/2] / [(2+√2)/4 - (√2-1)/2]\n   358\t          = [(√2 - 2(√2-1))/4] / [(2+√2 - 2(√2-1))/4]\n   359\t          = (√2 - 2√2 + 2) / (2+√2-2√2+2)\n   360\t          = (2-√2) / (4-√2) ✓\n   361\t\n   362\tslope(FP) = [(3√2-2)/8 - (√2-1)/2] / [3√2/8 - (√2-1)/2]\n   363\t          = [(3√2-2 - 4(√2-1))/8] / [(3√2 - 4(√2-1))/8]\n   364\t          = (3√2-2-4√2+4) / (3√2-4√2+4)\n   365\t          = (2-√2) / (4-√2) ✓\n   366\t\n   367\tSo slope(FP) = slope(FM). F, M, P are collinear. ✓\n   368\t\n   369\t### Example 6: Missing justification for point on circle\n   370\t\n   371\t**Claim:** \"Since A_k is constructed as the intersection of the supporting line at A_{k-1} with circle ω_k, it lies on circle ω_k.\"\n   372\t\n   373\t**Analysis:** By construction, A_k = supporting line ∩ ω_k, so A_k ∈ ω_k. But does A_k ∈ Ω (the larger circle)? That is a SEPARATE claim requiring separate justification. If the proof asserts A_k ∈ Ω without showing either the coordinates of A_k satisfy Ω's equation or an angle/power argument shows A_k ∈ Ω, this is a gap. ✓ Correctly evaluating this requires checking whether the proof provides such a justification.\n   374\t\n   375\t---\n   376\t\n   377\t## Part IX: Specific Problem Type Guidance\n   378\t\n   379\t### 9.1 Concurrence of Three Lines (e.g., ZE, CO, FM)\n   380\t\n   381\tFor a proof that three lines are concurrent, the minimum valid structure is:\n   382\t1. Find the intersection P of two of the lines\n   383\t2. Show P lies on the third line\n   384\t\n   385\tIn a coordinate proof, this means:\n   386\t- Solve the system for two lines to get explicit (x,y) coordinates of P\n   387\t- Substitute into the equation of the third line and verify the equation holds\n   388\t- OR: show that the slope from P to one point on the third line equals the slope of the third line\n   389\t\n   390\t**Evaluation criterion:** If a coordinate proof finds P and claims P lies on line FM \"by similar computation,\" the reader must be able to fill in this computation. If the computation is absent but the conclusion is stated, this is an incomplete but potentially valid proof (minor omission, routine verification). If the computation would actually FAIL (i.e., slope(FP) ≠ slope(FM) due to an arithmetic error earlier), then the entire proof is invalid.\n   391\t\n   392\t### 9.2 Arcs and Sub-Arc Identification\n   393\t\n   394\tWhen problem states \"Z lies on arc BC\" and \"E lies on arc AC,\" these constraints determine which of the two intersection points of the small circle with the circumcircle is Z and which is E. A proof that just computes the two intersection points without verifying which is on which arc is incomplete.\n   395\t\n   396\t**Evaluation criterion:** Verify that the proof either:\n   397\t- Shows explicit coordinates for E and Z and checks which arc each lies on\n   398\t- Provides a geometric argument (e.g., Z is closer to B than to A, hence on arc BC)\n   399\t- Notes that the specific identification doesn't affect the concurrence conclusion\n   400\t\n   401\t### 9.3 Rotation Angle Determination\n   402\t\n   403\tIn a rotation proof for a geometry problem:\n   404\t1. Identify the center of rotation (often a specific vertex or special point)\n   405\t2. The angle of rotation comes from the geometry: for example, if two segments subtend equal angles at a common point, the rotation maps one to the other\n   406\t3. The direction (clockwise/counterclockwise) must be consistent with the configuration\n   407\t\n   408\t**Checking angle = π (180°):** A rotation by π about a center O maps P to 2O-P (the point symmetric to P through O). This is valid only if the problem's geometric structure has a 180° symmetry. The angle π is correct if:\n   409\t- The center is the midpoint of some key segment\n   410\t- Two triangles are identified as centrally symmetric\n   411\t- Some other explicit π-rotation symmetry is established\n   412\t\n   413\tIf instead the correct angle should be, e.g., π/2, and the proof uses π, then images of all points under the rotation are wrong.\n   414\t\n   415\t---\n   416\t\n   417\t## Part X: Evaluation Rubric Summary for Geometry Proofs\n   418\t\n   419\t1. **Angle equalities:** Every claimed angle equality must cite a theorem (inscribed angle, tangent-chord, parallel lines, vertical angles, or supplementary angles). An unjustified equality is a material issue.\n   420\t\n   421\t2. **Concyclicity:** Claims that four points are concyclic must be established (angle sum = 180°, equal inscribed angles, power of point = 0, all equidistant from a center).\n   422\t\n   423\t3. **Arithmetic correctness (for coordinate proofs):** Every calculation with specific coordinates must be verified. An arithmetic error that produces a wrong coordinate invalidates all subsequent claims using that coordinate.\n   424\t\n   425\t4. **Rotation angle:** In rotation arguments, the angle must be explicitly derived from the given data and correctly computed.\n   426\t\n   427\t5. **Third point on circle:** A claim that a specific point lies on a circle must be justified either algebraically (satisfies the circle's equation) or geometrically (angle condition, power condition, etc.).\n   428\t\n   429\t6. **Concurrence conclusion:** The proof must actually establish concurrence, not just set up a rotation/inversion and stop before the final conclusion.\n   430\t\n   431\t**First material issue identification:** The first place in the proof where one of the above criteria fails — an unjustified claim, a wrong calculation, or a missing verification — is the first material issue to report.\n   432\t\n   433\t\n   434\t---\n   435\t\n   436\t## Appendix A: Extended Worked Examples — Geometry Proof Evaluation\n   437\t\n   438\t### A.1 Angle-Chase Evaluation — 25 Scenarios\n   439\t\n   440\t**Scenario 1 (Correct).** \"Let ABCD be a cyclic quadrilateral. Then ∠DAB + ∠BCD = 180°.\"\n   441\tProof: ∠DAB and ∠BCD are opposite angles of the cyclic quadrilateral. They subtend arcs BCD and DAB respectively (the full circle minus each arc). Since arc(BCD) + arc(DAB) = 360°, and inscribed angles are half their arcs: ∠DAB + ∠BCD = (arc BCD + arc DAB)/2 = 360°/2 = 180°. CORRECT. ✓\n   442\t\n   443\t**Scenario 2 (Incorrect — invalid concyclicity claim).** \"Points P,Q,R,S lie on a circle since ∠PQR = 60° and ∠PSR = 60°.\"\n   444\tAssessment: For four points to be concyclic with ∠PQR = ∠PSR, Q and S must be on the SAME SIDE of PR. The claim doesn't specify the configuration. Moreover, just knowing both angles are 60° doesn't by itself prove concyclicity without verifying they subtend the same arc. But actually: by the converse of the inscribed angle theorem, if ∠PQR = ∠PSR and Q,S lie on the same side of PR, then P,Q,R,S are concyclic. Without the same-side condition, this could fail (e.g., Q and S on opposite sides gives ∠PQR + ∠PSR = 180°, so PQRS is cyclic but Q and S are on opposite arcs). So the claim needs the same-side condition explicitly stated.\n   445\t\n   446\t**Scenario 3 (Correct).** \"By the inscribed angle theorem, ∠ACB = ∠ADB since both are inscribed in circle ω and subtend arc AB not containing C and D, where C and D are on the same arc.\"\n   447\tCORRECT, assuming the same-arc condition is verified.\n   448\t\n   449\t**Scenario 4 (Incorrect — direction of implication).** \"∠ACB = 90° implies A, C, B lie on a circle with AB as diameter.\"\n   450\tAssessment: This is CORRECT — the converse of Thales' theorem. If ∠ACB = 90°, then C lies on the circle with AB as diameter.\n   451\t\n   452\t**Scenario 5 (Incorrect — wrong tangent-chord formula).** \"The angle between tangent t at P and chord PQ equals ∠PRQ where R is any point on the arc PQ not containing the tangent point.\"\n   453\tAssessment: The tangent-chord angle equals the inscribed angle in the ALTERNATE segment. So the angle between t and PQ at P equals the inscribed angle in the arc on the OTHER SIDE of PQ from the tangent direction. CORRECT as stated if \"alternate segment\" is understood.\n   454\t\n   455\t**Scenario 6 (Correct — directed angle computation).** \"∠(AB, CD) = ∠(AB, EF) + ∠(EF, CD) by linearity of directed angles.\"\n   456\tCORRECT. Directed angles mod 180° satisfy: ∠(AB,CD) = ∠(AB,EF)+∠(EF,CD) for any line EF. This is just the additive property of angles.\n   457\t\n   458\t**Scenario 7 (Incorrect — mixing directed and undirected).** \"∠BAC = 30° and ∠BDC = 150°, so A,B,C,D are NOT concyclic.\"\n   459\tAssessment: Using directed angles mod 180°: ∠BAC and ∠BDC as directed angles: 30° and 150° are NOT congruent mod 180° (since 150° = 180°-30° and directed angle differences are taken mod 180°, 30° ≠ 150° mod 180°... wait: 150° mod 180° = 150°, and 30° mod 180° = 30°. These differ, so in directed angle sense, A,B,C,D are not concyclic. But in undirected sense: ∠BAC + ∠BDC = 30°+150° = 180°, so opposite angles of quadrilateral ABCD sum to 180°, meaning ABCD IS cyclic. The issue: are A and D on the same side of BC? If ∠BAC = 30° and ∠BDC = 30° they're on the same side (concyclic). If ∠BAC = 30° and ∠BDC = 150° = 180°-30°, they're on opposite sides of BC, and the QUADRILATERAL ABCD is cyclic (opposite angles sum to 180°). CORRECT — A,B,C,D ARE concyclic.\n   460\t\n   461\t**Scenario 8 (Incorrect — wrong power of point formula).** \"For point P outside circle with tangent PA and secant PBC: PA² = PB · PC.\"\n   462\tCORRECT (power of a point for external point). PA² = PB·PC. ✓\n   463\t\n   464\t**Scenario 9 (Incorrect — wrong power formula for internal point).** \"For point P inside circle with chords AB and CD through P: PA·PB = PC·PD.\"\n   465\tCORRECT. For intersecting chords, PA·PB = PC·PD (both equal the power of P). ✓\n   466\t\n   467\t**Scenario 10 (Incorrect — sign error in radical axis).** \"The radical axis of two circles is where they have equal power, so the equation of the radical axis is obtained by adding the circle equations.\"\n   468\tFIRST MATERIAL ISSUE: The radical axis is obtained by SUBTRACTING the circle equations (not adding). Subtracting: (x-h₁)²+(y-k₁)²-r₁² = (x-h₂)²+(y-k₂)²-r₂² gives a linear equation. Adding gives a quadratic (the \"coaxial\" circle through their intersection points). ✓ Subtraction gives the radical axis.\n   469\t\n   470\t### A.2 Coordinate Geometry Verification — 20 Scenarios\n   471\t\n   472\t**Scenario C1 (Correct).** \"For A=(0,0), B=(1,0), C=(√2/2, √2/2) with ∠A=45°: |AB|=1, |AC|=√(1/2+1/2)=1. Triangle is isosceles with AB=AC=1.\"\n   473\tCheck: |AC| = √((√2/2)²+(√2/2)²) = √(1/2+1/2) = √1 = 1. ✓ AB=AC=1. ✓\n   474\t\n   475\t**Scenario C2 (Correct).** \"Circumcenter O of A=(0,0), B=(1,0), C=(√2/2,√2/2): O lies on x=1/2 (perpendicular bisector of AB).\"\n   476\tCheck: Midpoint of AB is (1/2,0). AB is horizontal, so perpendicular bisector is vertical: x=1/2. ✓\n   477\t\n   478\t**Scenario C3 (Correct).** \"Perpendicular bisector of AC (from A=(0,0) to C=(√2/2,√2/2)): midpoint is (√2/4, √2/4), slope of AC is 1, so perp bisector has slope -1 and equation y-√2/4 = -(x-√2/4), i.e., y = -x+√2/2.\"\n   479\tCheck: slope of AC = (√2/2-0)/(√2/2-0) = 1. ✓ Perp slope = -1. ✓ Through (√2/4,√2/4): y-√2/4 = -(x-√2/4) → y = -x+√2/4+√2/4 = -x+√2/2. ✓\n   480\t\n   481\t**Scenario C4 (Correct).** \"Intersection of x=1/2 and y=-x+√2/2: y = -1/2+√2/2 = (√2-1)/2. So O=(1/2,(√2-1)/2).\"\n   482\tCheck: substitute x=1/2: y = -1/2+√2/2 = (√2-1)/2. ✓\n   483\t\n   484\t**Scenario C5 (Correct).** \"Verification |OA|²=|OB|²=|OC|²: |OA|²=(1/2)²+((√2-1)/2)²=(1+(√2-1)²)/4=(1+3-2√2)/4=(4-2√2)/4=(2-√2)/2. ✓\"\n   485\tDetailed check: (√2-1)² = 2-2√2+1 = 3-2√2. (1+(3-2√2))/4 = (4-2√2)/4 = (2-√2)/2. ✓\n   486\t|OB|²=(1/2-1)²+((√2-1)/2)²=1/4+(3-2√2)/4=(4-2√2)/4=(2-√2)/2. ✓\n   487\t|OC|²=(1/2-√2/2)²+((√2-1)/2-√2/2)²=((1-√2)/2)²+((√2-1-√2)/2)²=((1-√2)²+1)/4=(3-2√2+1)/4=(4-2√2)/4. ✓\n   488\t\n   489\t**Scenario C6 (Incorrect — arithmetic error).** \"D = foot of perpendicular from C=(√2/2,√2/2) to AB (x-axis): D = (√2/2, 0). CD = distance from C to x-axis = √2/2. Circle with center C, radius CD: (x-√2/2)²+(y-√2/2)² = 2.\" \n   490\tINCORRECT: CD = √2/2 so r = √2/2, r² = 1/2. The circle equation should be (x-√2/2)²+(y-√2/2)² = 1/2, NOT 2.\n   491\t\n   492\t**Scenario C7 (Correct).** \"F = intersection of y=x with (x-√2/2)²+(y-√2/2)²=1/2: substitute y=x to get 2(x-√2/2)²=1/2, (x-√2/2)²=1/4, x=√2/2±1/2. The internal point on segment AC is x=(√2-1)/2 (since (√2+1)/2 > √2/2 = |C|, would be outside AC).\"\n   493\tCheck: √2/2 ≈ 0.707. (√2+1)/2 ≈ 1.207 > 0.707, outside [0,√2/2]. (√2-1)/2 ≈ 0.207 ∈ [0,√2/2]. ✓ So F = ((√2-1)/2, (√2-1)/2). ✓\n   494\t\n   495\t**Scenario C8 (Correct).** \"Subtracting circumcircle equation from small circle equation gives radical axis: 2(√2/2-1/2)x+2(√2/2-(√2-1)/2)y = constant.\"\n   496\tMore explicitly: circumcircle: (x-1/2)²+(y-(√2-1)/2)² = (2-√2)/2. Small circle: (x-√2/2)²+(y-√2/2)² = 1/2.\n   497\tExpand both, subtract:\n   498\tCircumcircle: x²-x+1/4+y²-(√2-1)y+(√2-1)²/4 = (2-√2)/2.\n   499\tSmall circle: x²-√2x+1/2+y²-√2y+1/2 = 1/2.\n   500\tSubtract: (-1+√2)x+(-(√2-1)+√2)y+(1/4+(3-2√2)/4-1/2-1/2) = (2-√2)/2-1/2.\n   501\t= (√2-1)x+(1)y+... let me redo this carefully.\n   502\t\n   503\tExpanding circumcircle: x²-x+1/4+y²-(√2-1)y+(3-2√2)/4=(2-√2)/2.\n   504\tExpanding small circle: x²-√2x+1/2+y²-√2y+1/2=1/2.\n   505\t\n   506\tSubtract (small -
