This maps B↦C and C↦B, and A↦A'.\"\n   545\tCheck: Rotation by π about M: M is midpoint of BC, so B↦C and C↦B ✓. A maps to the point A' with M=(A+A')/2, so A'=2M-A. If M is NOT the midpoint of AA', then A'≠A.\n   546\t\n   547\t**Scenario R2 (Correct).** \"If rotating by angle θ about center O maps X to X' and Y to Y', then ∠XOY = ∠X'OY' = θ and |OX|=|OX'|, |OY|=|OY'|, |XY|=|X'Y'|.\"\n   548\tCORRECT. Rotations preserve distances and angles between any two points. ✓\n   549\t\n   550\t**Scenario R3 (Incorrect — wrong rotation angle in geometry proof).** \"In triangle ABC with ∠A=45°, rotate by angle π about the circumcenter O.\"\n   551\tAssessment: Rotation by π about O maps each point P to 2O-P (point symmetry). This maps A to the antipode A* of A on the circumcircle. The angle ∠A=45° does NOT imply a π-rotation is the right transformation. The rotation angle should be ∠BOC = 2∠BAC = 90° (central angle theorem), NOT π=180°.\n   552\t\n   553\t**Scenario R4 (Correct).** \"The rotation by ∠BOC = 2∠BAC about O maps arc BC to arc BC.\"\n   554\tCentral angle ∠BOC = 2·∠BAC (inscribed angle theorem). Rotation by ∠BOC about O: this is the rotation that \"moves along the circle\" by the arc BC. Points ON the circle would be mapped to other points on the circle. This is correct for the standard rotation in circle geometry.\n   555\t\n   556\t**Scenario R5 (Incorrect — incorrect images).** \"Rotation by π/2 about origin maps (a,b) to (-b,a).\" \n   557\tCheck: Rotation by π/2 counterclockwise: x'=x·cos(π/2)-y·sin(π/2)=-y, y'=x·sin(π/2)+y·cos(π/2)=x. So (a,b)↦(-b,a). ✓ CORRECT.\n   558\t\n   559\t**Scenario R6 (Correct).** \"Rotation by π about O=(h,k) maps P=(x,y) to P'=(2h-x, 2k-y).\"\n   560\tx' = (x-h)cos(π)-(y-k)sin(π)+h = -(x-h)+h = 2h-x. ✓\n   561\ty' = (x-h)sin(π)+(y-k)cos(π)+k = -(y-k)+k = 2k-y. ✓\n   562\t\n   563\t**Scenario R7 (Incorrect — missing case in rotation).** \"There are two possible rotation angles: θ and -θ. The proof only checks θ.\"\n   564\tIf the problem has a reflection symmetry (equilateral triangle, etc.), both θ and -θ (clockwise and counterclockwise) might be relevant. A proof that only checks one rotation may miss solutions or draw wrong conclusions.\n   565\t\n   566\t### A.4 Circle Geometry Patterns — 15 Scenarios\n   567\t\n   568\t**Scenario Ci1 (Correct — Ptolemy application).** \"For cyclic quadrilateral ABCD: AC·BD = AB·CD + AD·BC.\"\n   569\tPTOLEMY'S THEOREM. ✓\n   570\t\n   571\t**Scenario Ci2 (Correct — inversion application).** \"Inversion with center P and radius r maps circle Γ not through P to another circle Γ' (not through P). The image of lines through P are lines through P.\"\n   572\tCORRECT. Inversion swaps circles not through P with circles not through P, and swaps circles through P with lines not through P. ✓\n   573\t\n   574\t**Scenario Ci3 (Incorrect — wrong inversion formula).** \"Inversion with center O and radius r maps P=(a,b) to P'=(a/r², b/r²).\"\n   575\tINCORRECT. Inversion maps P to P' on ray OP with OP·OP'=r². If P=(a,b), then |OP|=√(a²+b²) and the image P'= (r²/(a²+b²))·(a,b). So P'=(r²a/(a²+b²), r²b/(a²+b²)). The formula (a/r², b/r²) is wrong.\n   576\t\n   577\t**Scenario Ci4 (Correct).** \"For a circle ω with center O and radius R, and a chord PQ, the perpendicular from O bisects PQ.\"\n   578\tCORRECT. The perpendicular from the center to a chord always bisects the chord (by symmetry). ✓\n   579\t\n   580\t**Scenario Ci5 (Correct).** \"Two circles are internally tangent at T if and only if their centers O₁,O₂ and T are collinear (T between O₁ and O₂ for external tangency, T on the extension for internal tangency).\"\n   581\tCORRECT for tangency. For internal tangency (one circle inside the other): |O₁O₂| = |r₁-r₂| and the tangent point T lies on segment O₁O₂ (extended if needed). ✓\n   582\t\n   583\t### A.5 Collinearity and Concurrence Patterns\n   584\t\n   585\t**Pattern Col1 (Correct).** \"Three points A=(x₁,y₁), B=(x₂,y₂), C=(x₃,y₃) are collinear iff the matrix [[x₁,y₁,1],[x₂,y₂,1],[x₃,y₃,1]] has determinant 0.\"\n   586\tCORRECT. The determinant condition: x₁(y₂-y₃)+x₂(y₃-y₁)+x₃(y₁-y₂)=0. ✓\n   587\t\n   588\t**Pattern Con1 (Correct).** \"Lines L₁: a₁x+b₁y=c₁, L₂: a₂x+b₂y=c₂, L₃: a₃x+b₃y=c₃ are concurrent iff their system has a solution, i.e., det([[a₁,b₁,c₁],[a₂,b₂,c₂],[a₃,b₃,c₃]])=0.\"\n   589\tCORRECT. The three lines meet at a single point iff the 3×3 determinant with the line equation coefficients vanishes. ✓\n   590\t\n   591\t**Pattern Con2 (Ceva's theorem check).** For triangle ABC with D on BC, E on CA, F on AB: AD, BE, CF concurrent iff (BD/DC)·(CE/EA)·(AF/FB)=1 (Ceva's theorem with signed ratios).\n   592\t\n   593\t**Pattern Col2 (Slope method).** Points P,Q,R collinear iff slope(PQ)=slope(QR). Requires Q≠P and Q≠R and Q not at infinity.\n   594\t\n   595\t---\n   596\t\n   597\t## Appendix B: Competition-Specific Geometry Notes\n   598\t\n   599\t### B.1 BMOSL Geometry Problems\n   600\t\n   601\tBMOSL (British Mathematical Olympiad Shortlist) geometry problems typically involve:\n   602\t- Cyclic quadrilaterals and concyclic points\n   603\t- Angle chasing with directed angles\n   604\t- Coordinate geometry for construction verification\n   605\t- The olympiad standard: all steps must be justified, but \"standard facts\" (inscribed angle theorem, power of a point) can be cited without re-proof\n   606\t\n   607\t### B.2 USAMO Geometry Problems\n   608\t\n   609\tUSAMO geometry problems are generally harder and may require:\n   610\t- Projective geometry (cross-ratio, harmonic ranges)\n   611\t- Advanced circle theorems (Miquel's theorem, radical axes)\n   612\t- Sophisticated inversion arguments\n   613\t- Rigorous handling of degenerate cases\n   614\t\n   615\t### B.3 Evaluation Standard for Competition Geometry\n   616\t\n   617\tAt competition level, the following are considered \"standard\" and do NOT need proof:\n   618\t- Inscribed angle theorem\n   619\t- Power of a point\n   620\t- Ceva's theorem and Menelaus's theorem\n   621\t- Basic trigonometric identities (law of sines, law of cosines)\n   622\t- Ptolemy's theorem\n   623\t\n   624\tThe following DO need justification even if \"standard\":\n   625\t- That specific points are concyclic (must establish which condition holds)\n   626\t- That a point lies on a specific circle (must verify one of the conditions)\n   627\t- That a rotation maps one specific point to another (must verify distance and angle)\n   628\t\n   629\t---\n   630\t\n   631\t## Appendix C: Extended Reference — Angles in Complex Numbers\n   632\t\n   633\tFor geometry in the complex plane, a point P corresponds to complex number z_P. Key facts:\n   634\t\n   635\t**Rotation:** Rotation by angle θ about origin multiplies by e^{iθ}.\n   636\t\n   637\t**Reflection:** Reflection over real axis: z ↦ z̄. Reflection over line through origin at angle α: z ↦ e^{2iα}z̄.\n   638\t\n   639\t**Collinearity:** A, B, C collinear iff (C-A)/(B-A) is real.\n   640\t\n   641\t**Concyclicity:** A, B, C, D concyclic (or collinear) iff (A-C)(B-D)/((A-D)(B-C)) is real (cross-ratio is real).\n   642\t\n   643\t**Angle:** Directed angle ∠(AB,AC) = arg((C-A)/(B-A)) mod π.\n   644\t\n   645\tThese formulas provide an algebraic framework for verifying geometric claims. When a coordinate proof is too computational, complex number methods can simplify.\n   646\t\n   647\t---\n   648\t\n   649\t*End of Geometry Background Reference*\n   650\t\n   651\t\n   652\t---\n   653\t\n   654\t## Appendix D: Extended Geometry Problem Patterns\n   655\t\n   656\t### D.1 Angle-Chase Reference — Additional Patterns\n   657\t\n   658\t**AC1 (Directed angle formula).** For points A,B,C,D with ABCD concyclic: angle(AC,BC) = angle(AD,BD) as directed angles mod 180 degrees. This is the key rule for angle chasing.\n   659\t\n   660\t**AC2 (Central angle rule).** Central angle = 2 * inscribed angle subtending same arc. angle(AOB) = 2 * angle(ACB) where O is center and C is on the circle.\n   661\t\n   662\t**AC3 (Tangent-chord angle).** Angle between tangent at P and chord PQ equals angle subtended by arc PQ in the alternate segment.\n   663\t\n   664\t**AC4 (Alternate segment theorem).** If T is on circle omega and l is tangent to omega at T, and AB is a chord, then angle(l, AT) = angle(ABT) where the angle is in the alternate segment.\n   665\t\n   666\t**AC5 (Cyclic polygon).** In a cyclic polygon with n sides, the sum of alternating exterior angles = 180 degrees. For cyclic quadrilateral: opposite angles sum to 180 degrees.\n   667\t\n   668\t### D.2 Coordinate Computation Reference — Standard Formulas\n   669\t\n   670\t**Distance formula:** |PQ|^2 = (x_Q - x_P)^2 + (y_Q - y_P)^2.\n   671\t\n   672\t**Midpoint formula:** M = ((x_P+x_Q)/2, (y_P+y_Q)/2).\n   673\t\n   674\t**Line through P=(x_1,y_1) and Q=(x_2,y_2):** (y-y_1)/(x-x_1) = (y_2-y_1)/(x_2-x_1) if x_1 != x_2. Or: (x_2-x_1)(y-y_1) = (y_2-y_1)(x-x_1).\n   675\t\n   676\t**Circumcenter:** For triangle with vertices A,B,C, the circumcenter O satisfies |OA|=|OB|=|OC|. Found by intersecting perpendicular bisectors of any two sides.\n   677\t\n   678\t**Circumradius formula:** R = |AB|/(2*sin(angle(ACB))) by law of sines.\n   679\t\n   680\t**Standard surds:**\n   681\t- sqrt(2) ≈ 1.41421\n   682\t- sqrt(2)/2 ≈ 0.70711\n   683\t- sqrt(2)-1 ≈ 0.41421\n   684\t- sqrt(2)+1 ≈ 2.41421\n   685\t- (sqrt(2)-1)/2 ≈ 0.20711\n   686\t- (sqrt(2)+1)/2 ≈ 1.20711\n   687\t- (3*sqrt(2)-2)/8 ≈ 0.28033\n   688\t- 3*sqrt(2)/8 ≈ 0.53033\n   689\t\n   690\tThese approximate values help verify that computed points are in expected relative positions.\n   691\t\n   692\t### D.3 Rotation Proof Methodology\n   693\t\n   694\t**Step-by-step rotation proof structure:**\n   695\t\n   696\t1. **Identify rotation center:** Usually a vertex, circumcenter, or special point.\n   697\t2. **Determine rotation angle:** From the geometric data (e.g., central angle, polygon angle).\n   698\t3. **Compute images:** Use rotation formula or geometric argument.\n   699\t4. **Establish claimed equalities:** Show the image of a specific segment/angle equals another.\n   700\t5. **Conclude the geometric claim:** (concurrence, collinearity, etc.) from the rotation.\n   701\t\n   702\t**Rotation angle errors are common.** The angle is typically:\n   703\t- pi/n for regular n-gon\n   704\t- 2*angle_A for central angle at vertex A\n   705\t- Angle of some inscribed figure\n   706\t\n   707\tIf the proof states angle = pi without deriving this from the problem, check whether the configuration really has 180-degree rotational symmetry.\n   708\t\n   709\t### D.4 Circle Tangency and Intersection Patterns\n   710\t\n   711\t**Internal tangency:** Two circles internally tangent at T: centers O1, O2 with |O1O2| = |r1-r2|. The tangent at T is the common internal tangent.\n   712\t\n   713\t**External tangency:** |O1O2| = r1+r2. Tangent point T lies on segment O1O2.\n   714\t\n   715\t**Radical axis of two intersecting circles:** If they intersect at P and Q, the radical axis is line PQ.\n   716\t\n   717\t**Finding angle subtended by chord at center:** If chord has length c in circle of radius r: angle = 2*arcsin(c/(2r)).\n   718\t\n   719\t### D.5 Trigonometric Identities in Geometry\n   720\t\n   721\t**Law of sines:** a/sin(A) = b/sin(B) = c/sin(C) = 2R.\n   722\t\n   723\t**Law of cosines:** c^2 = a^2+b^2-2ab*cos(C).\n   724\t\n   725\t**Extended law of sines:** a = 2R*sin(A) where R is circumradius.\n   726\t\n   727\t**Area formula:** [ABC] = (1/2)ab*sin(C) = (abc)/(4R).\n   728\t\n   729\t**Stewart's theorem:** For cevian d from A to side BC at point D with BD=m, DC=n: b^2*m+c^2*n = a(d^2+mn).\n   730\t\n   731\t**Ptolemy (generalized):** For cyclic ABCD: AC*BD = AB*CD + AD*BC.\n   732\t\n   733\t### D.6 Combinatorial Geometry Patterns\n   734\t\n   735\t**Support line:** A line l is a support line of convex set K at point p in K if l contains p and K lies entirely in one closed half-plane bounded by l.\n   736\t\n   737\t**Support point:** A point p on the boundary of convex K is a support point if there exists a support line at p.\n   738\t\n   739\t**Evaluation criterion for support point arguments:** A claim that \"point A_k is a support point\" must be substantiated. Options:\n   740\t- Show A_k is on the boundary of the convex hull\n   741\t- Show there exists a support line at A_k\n   742\t- Use the definition directly (A_k is on a tangent line with all other points on one side)\n   743\t\n   744\tIf a proof claims A_k lies on a specific circle because \"it would be a support point otherwise,\" this reasoning is BACKWARDS unless the support property has been established first.\n   745\t\n   746\t**Inscribed polygon in circle:** A polygon inscribed in circle omega has all vertices on omega. A point P lies on omega iff |P - O| = r (where O is center, r is radius).\n   747\t\n   748\t**Point on circle verification methods:**\n   749\t1. Show |P-O|^2 = r^2 (coordinate check)\n   750\t2. Show ∠PAQ = ∠POQ/2 for some chord AQ (inscribed angle)\n   751\t3. Show the power of P with respect to omega equals 0\n   752\t\n   753\t### D.7 Proof of Concurrence — Methods\n   754\t\n   755\t**Ceva's theorem:** In triangle ABC with cevians AD, BE, CF (D on BC, E on CA, F on AB): concurrent iff (BD/DC)*(CE/EA)*(AF/FB) = 1.\n   756\t\n   757\t**Radical axis:** Three radical axes of three circles are concurrent at the radical center.\n   758\t\n   759\t**Perspective triangles:** Triangles ABC and A'B'C' are perspective from a point iff AA', BB', CC' are concurrent (lines through corresponding vertices meet at one point).\n   760\t\n   761\t**Coordinate method:** Find intersection of two lines; verify on third.\n   762\t\n   763\t**Vector method:** Express each line as P = A + t*(B-A) and solve for t. Three lines concurrent iff the system is consistent.\n   764\t\n   765\t### D.8 Special Lines and Points\n   766\t\n   767\t**Euler line:** In any non-equilateral triangle, the circumcenter O, centroid G, and orthocenter H are collinear. OG:GH = 1:2.\n   768\t\n   769\t**Nine-point circle:** The midpoints of sides, feet of altitudes, and midpoints of segments from vertices to orthocenter all lie on one circle (nine-point circle) of radius R/2.\n   770\t\n   771\t**Simson line:** For a point P on the circumcircle of triangle ABC, the projections of P onto the sides of ABC are collinear (Simson line).\n   772\t\n   773\t**Polar-pole:** For a circle omega with center O and radius r, the polar of point P (with respect to omega) is the line perpendicular to OP at the inverse point of P.\n   774\t\n   775\t*End of Geometry Extended Appendix*\n","message":"775 lines read from file starting from line 1. A one-to-one correspondence must be established clearly.\n    22\t\n    23\t### 1.2 Double Counting\n    24\t\n    25\t**Method:** Count a set S of pairs (object, property) in two ways:\n    26\t- By object: Σ_{x ∈ X} (number of properties x has)\n    27\t- By property: Σ_{P ∈ Properties} (number of objects with property P)\n    28\t\n    29\tBoth sides equal |S|.\n    30\t\n    31\t**Evaluation criterion:** The set S must be clearly defined. Both counting methods must correctly enumerate elements of S without over- or under-counting.\n    32\t\n    33\t### 1.3 Stars and Bars\n    34\t\n    35\tThe number of ways to write n as an ordered sum of k non-negative integers is C(n+k-1, k-1). This counts solutions to x₁+x₂+...+xₖ = n with xᵢ ≥ 0.\n    36\t\n    37\tFor positive integers (xᵢ ≥ 1): substitute yᵢ = xᵢ-1, getting y₁+...+yₖ = n-k, count C(n-1, k-1).\n    38\t\n    39\t### 1.4 Inclusion-Exclusion\n    40\t\n    41\t|A₁∪A₂∪...∪Aₙ| = Σ|Aᵢ| - Σ|Aᵢ∩Aⱼ| + Σ|Aᵢ∩Aⱼ∩Aₖ| - ... + (-1)^{n+1}|A₁∩...∩Aₙ|\n    42\t\n    43\t**Derangements:** D(n) = n! Σ_{k=0}^n (-1)^k/k! = n!(1-1+1/2!-1/3!+...) ≈ n!/e.\n    44\t\n    45\t---\n    46\t\n    47\t## Part II: Graph Theory and Combinatorial Structures\n    48\t\n    49\t### 2.1 Graph Theory Basics\n    50\t\n    51\tA graph G = (V, E) with vertex set V and edge set E. Key properties:\n    52\t- Simple graph: no multi-edges, no self-loops\n    53\t- Complete graph K_n: every pair of vertices connected\n    54\t- Bipartite graph K_{m,n}: vertices split into two groups, edges only between groups\n    55\t- Tree: connected graph with no cycles; has exactly n-1 edges for n vertices\n    56\t\n    57\t**Pigeonhole principle:** If n objects are placed in k bins and n > k, at least one bin has at least ⌈n/k⌉ objects.\n    58\t\n    59\t**Handshaking lemma:** Σ_{v∈V} deg(v) = 2|E|.\n    60\t\n    61\t### 2.2 Matchings and Hall's Theorem\n    62\t\n    63\tA **matching** in a bipartite graph is a set of edges with no shared vertices.\n    64\t\n    65\t**Hall's marriage theorem:** A bipartite graph G = (A∪B, E) has a matching saturating all of A if and only if for every subset S ⊆ A, |N(S)| ≥ |S| (where N(S) is the neighborhood of S in B).\n    66\t\n    67\t**Perfect matching:** Matches every vertex. In K_{n,n}, there are n! perfect matchings.\n    68\t\n    69\t**Evaluation criterion:** When a proof claims a matching or bijection exists, it must either exhibit one explicitly or invoke Hall's theorem with verification of the Hall condition.\n    70\t\n    71\t---\n    72\t\n    73\t## Part III: Boolean Lattice and Subset Systems\n    74\t\n    75\t### 3.1 The Boolean Lattice\n    76\t\n    77\tThe power set 2^S of a set S = {1,...,n} with the partial order of inclusion forms the Boolean lattice B_n. Key properties:\n    78\t- |B_n| = 2^n\n    79\t- Ranked by cardinality: rank k level has C(n,k) elements\n    80\t- Graded poset with n+1 ranks\n    81\t\n    82\t**Intervals in the Boolean lattice:** For A ⊆ B ⊆ S, the interval [A,B] = {C : A⊆C⊆B} is isomorphic to B_{|B|-|A|} and has 2^{|B|-|A|} elements.\n    83\t\n    84\t### 3.2 Chains and Antichains\n    85\t\n    86\tA **chain** is a totally ordered subset (any two elements comparable). A **antichain** is a set of mutually incomparable elements (no containments).\n    87\t\n    88\t**Dilworth's theorem:** The minimum number of chains needed to cover the poset equals the maximum antichain size.\n    89\t\n    90\t**Sperner's theorem:** The maximum antichain in B_n has size C(n, ⌊n/2⌋) (take all subsets of size ⌊n/2⌋).\n    91\t\n    92\t### 3.3 Multiplicative Functions on the Boolean Lattice\n    93\t\n    94\tA function f: 2^S → ℝ is **multiplicative** over (∪, ∩) if f(T₁)·f(T₂) = f(T₁∪T₂)·f(T₁∩T₂) for all T₁,T₂ ⊆ S.\n    95\t\n    96\tThis is a strong structural condition. Not every function satisfying this for \"many pairs\" satisfies it for all pairs.\n    97\t\n    98\t**Evaluation criterion:** A proof that f satisfies the multiplicative condition must verify it for ALL pairs (T₁, T₂), not just specific families. The verification \"one checks by routine computation\" is a placeholder that must be backed by an actual argument. If the proof leaves this verification implicit and it is genuinely non-trivial, this constitutes a critical skipped step.\n    99\t\n   100\t**Characterization theorem:** The non-negative functions f: 2^S → ℝ satisfying f(T₁)f(T₂) = f(T₁∪T₂)f(T₁∩T₂) are exactly those of the form f(T) = 2^{|T∩A|} for some A⊆S (when f(∅)=1), or f(T) = χ_{[U,U∪A']}(T) extended multiplicatively (when f(∅)=0).\n   101\t\n   102\t### 3.4 Möbius Inversion on the Boolean Lattice\n   103\t\n   104\tThe Möbius function for the Boolean lattice B_n is μ(A,B) = (-1)^{|B|-|A|} for A ⊆ B.\n   105\t\n   106\t**Inversion formula:** If g(T) = Σ_{U⊆T} f(U) for all T, then f(T) = Σ_{U⊆T} (-1)^{|T|-|U|} g(U).\n   107\t\n   108\t**Application to blue-set counting:** If f(T) = #{blue subsets of T}, then the indicator of whether T itself is blue is:\n   109\t1_B(T) = Σ_{U⊆T} (-1)^{|T|-|U|} f(U)\n   110\t\n   111\tThis is the Möbius inversion of f (where f(T) = Σ_{U∈B, U⊆T} 1 = Σ_{U⊆T} 1_B(U) is the cumulative count).\n   112\t\n   113\t**Evaluation criterion:** A proof using Möbius inversion must correctly state and apply the inversion formula. An error in the formula (wrong signs, wrong indexing) is a material issue. However, the inversion step itself is \"routine\" and may be cited as a known fact without re-proof in a competition context.\n   114\t\n   115\t---\n   116\t\n   117\t## Part IV: Extremal Combinatorics\n   118\t\n   119\t### 4.1 Optimization with Distinct Elements\n   120\t\n   121\t**Basic setup:** Given n distinct positive integers a₁ < a₂ < ... < aₙ, find the minimum value of max(Σ_{i∈I} aᵢ) or other extremal quantities subject to conditions on subset sums.\n   122\t\n   123\t**Key principle:** For k-element subsets, the one with the largest sum consists of the k largest elements {a_{n-k+1}, ..., aₙ}. The one with the smallest sum consists of the k smallest elements {a₁, ..., aₖ}.\n   124\t\n   125\t**Common technique:** Set a_{m} = c (the \"median\" element), then use inequalities:\n   126\t- a_{m-1} ≤ c-1, a_{m-2} ≤ c-2, ..., a_{m-k} ≤ c-k\n   127\t- a_{m+1} ≥ c+1, a_{m+2} ≥ c+2, ..., a_{m+k} ≥ c+k\n   128\t\n   129\tThese give lower and upper bounds on partial sums, which then constrain the possible values of c.\n   130\t\n   131\t### 4.2 Minimality Arguments\n   132\t\n   133\t**Technique:** To show that a minimum value of some parameter is N:\n   134\t1. **Existence:** Construct a configuration achieving the value N\n   135\t2. **Lower bound:** Show no configuration achieves a value less than N\n   136\t\n   137\tFor (1), the construction must be verified to satisfy ALL the problem's conditions. For (2), the argument must work for GENERAL configurations satisfying the problem's conditions, not just special cases.\n   138\t\n   139\t**Evaluation criterion:** A proof that omits the existence direction (just shows the lower bound) proves an upper bound on the minimum, not the minimum itself. A proof that omits the lower bound just gives an example. Both directions are REQUIRED for a complete proof.\n   140\t\n   141\t### 4.3 The Role of Strict vs Non-Strict Inequalities\n   142\t\n   143\tWhen bounding a sum sum_{i=1}^k a_i ≤ kc - k(k+1)/2 (where c is the (k+1)-th element and the elements are distinct integers), the bound comes from:\n   144\ta₁ ≤ c-k, a₂ ≤ c-k+1, ..., aₖ ≤ c-1, so sum ≤ Σᵢ₌₁ᵏ (c-k+i-1) = kc - k(k+1)/2.\n   145\t\n   146\tThis is a UPPER BOUND. The lower bound for a_{k+1},...,a_{2k} uses: a_{k+1+j} ≥ c+j for j=1,...,k.\n   147\t\n   148\t**Evaluation criterion:** Strict vs non-strict inequalities can change the answer by 1. In a minimality problem, if the minimum involves \"≥\" vs \">\" or \"=\" vs \"≤\", check that the proof's inequalities are of the right type.\n   149\t\n   150\t---\n   151\t\n   152\t## Part V: Combinatorial Designs and Constructions\n   153\t\n   154\t### 5.1 Existence Proofs via Explicit Construction\n   155\t\n   156\tWhen a problem asks to show a configuration exists with given properties, the proof must:\n   157\t1. Exhibit an explicit configuration\n   158\t2. Verify EVERY required property for that configuration\n   159\t3. (If asking for minimum) show no smaller/fewer configuration satisfies all properties\n   160\t\n   161\t**Common error:** Exhibiting a configuration and checking only a subset of the required properties, assuming the rest hold.\n   162\t\n   163\t### 5.2 General vs Special Case\n   164\t\n   165\tIf a problem asks to show that for all n (or all k), some property holds, the proof must work for GENERAL n (or k). A proof that only handles specific cases (e.g., n = 1, 2, 3) and extrapolates without justification has a critical gap.\n   166\t\n   167\t**Evaluation criterion:** If the proof shows existence/construction for specific values and claims \"similarly for other values\" or \"by analogous argument,\" check whether the argument is genuinely analogous. If the analogous argument requires non-trivial new ideas, citing it without providing it is a gap.\n   168\t\n   169\t### 5.3 Counting Configurations\n   170\t\n   171\tWhen counting configurations with multiple constraints:\n   172\t\n   173\t**Strategy 1 (Sequential):** Count the number of ways to make each choice in sequence, multiplying at each step (only if choices are independent or conditional counts are computed).\n   174\t\n   175\t**Strategy 2 (Characterize then count):** First show all valid configurations have a specific structural form, then count that form.\n   176\t\n   177\t**Evaluation criterion for Strategy 2:** The \"characterize\" step must be bidirectional:\n   178\t- (⇒) Every valid configuration has the claimed form\n   179\t- (⇐) Every configuration of the claimed form is valid\n   180\t\n   181\tMissing either direction means the count is wrong (either over-counting or under-counting).\n   182\t\n   183\t---\n   184\t\n   185\t## Part VI: Set Systems and Monotone Functions\n   186\t\n   187\t### 6.1 Intersecting Families\n   188\t\n   189\tA family F of sets is **intersecting** if any two members of F have a non-empty intersection. Equivalently, no two members are disjoint.\n   190\t\n   191\t**Key result:** Any intersecting antichain in 2^S has size ≤ C(n-1, ⌊(n-1)/2⌋) (Erdős-Ko-Rado theorem for uniform families).\n   192\t\n   193\tIn the context of proof evaluation, an intersecting family arises naturally from functional conditions like f(T₁)f(T₂) = f(T₁∪T₂)f(T₁∩T₂) with f(∅) = 0: this forces any two sets in the support of f to intersect.\n   194\t\n   195\t### 6.2 Upward-Closed and Downward-Closed Families\n   196\t\n   197\tA family F is **upward-closed (upset)** if whenever A ∈ F and A ⊆ B ⊆ S, then B ∈ F.\n   198\tA family F is **downward-closed (downset)** if whenever A ∈ F and B ⊆ A, then B ∈ F.\n   199\t\n   200\t**Evaluation criterion:** When a proof concludes that the support of a function is upward-closed from F = {T : T ⊇ U} for some minimal element U, this conclusion requires verifying:\n   201\t- U is indeed in F (f(U) > 0)\n   202\t- U is minimal in F (f(V) = 0 for all proper subsets V ⊊ U)\n   203\t- Any superset of U is in F (f(T) > 0 for all T ⊇ U)\n   204\t\n   205\tThe third point requires explicit verification from the functional condition.\n   206\t\n   207\t### 6.3 Chains in the Boolean Lattice and Order Ideals\n   208\t\n   209\tA **chain** A₀ ⊂ A₁ ⊂ ... ⊂ Aₙ with |Aᵢ| = i is called a **maximal chain** in B_n (since it has length n+1, the maximum possible). The number of maximal chains in B_n is n! (corresponding to the n! orderings of the elements of S).\n   210\t\n   211\t**Application:** When counting pairs of disjoint chains (A₀ ⊂ A₁ ⊂ ... ⊂ Aₙ) and (B₀ ⊂ B₁ ⊂ ... ⊂ Bₙ) with A_n ∩ B_n = ∅ in B_{2n}:\n   212\t1. Choose A_n: C(2n, n) ways (any n-element subset)\n   213\t2. B_n is forced (= complement of A_n since |B_n| = n and A_n ∩ B_n = ∅ and A_n ∪ B_n = {1,...,2n} when n+n = 2n)\n   214\t3. Build the chain inside A_n: n! ways\n   215\t4. Build the chain inside B_n: n! ways\n   216\tTotal: C(2n,n)·n!·n! = (2n)!\n   217\t\n   218\t---\n   219\t\n   220\t## Part VII: Common Proof Structures in Combinatorics Problems\n   221\t\n   222\t### 7.1 Greedy Arguments\n   223\t\n   224\tA greedy algorithm makes locally optimal choices. A greedy proof must:\n   225\t1. Define the greedy choice precisely\n   226\t2. Show the greedy choice doesn't preclude future valid choices (exchange argument or augmenting path argument)\n   227\t3. Show the result of the greedy algorithm has the required properties\n   228\t\n   229\t**Common error:** Assuming greedy is optimal without proving the exchange argument.\n   230\t\n   231\t### 7.2 Induction on Problem Parameters\n   232\t\n   233\t**Base case:** Verify the claim for the smallest valid parameter (n=1, k=0, etc.).\n   234\t\n   235\t**Inductive step:** Assume the claim for all values ≤ n-1, prove for n. The reduction from n to n-1 (or n-k) must be carefully justified.\n   236\t\n   237\t**Evaluation criterion:** If the inductive step says \"apply the induction hypothesis,\" verify that:\n   238\t- The hypothesis applies (the smaller problem satisfies the induction's hypotheses)\n   239\t- The conclusion for the smaller problem correctly implies the conclusion for the larger\n   240\t\n   241\t### 7.3 Extremal Arguments\n   242\t\n   243\t**Setup:** Take an element/configuration with a specific extremal property (minimal, maximal, etc.).\n   244\t\n   245\t**Technique:** Use the extremality to derive structural properties.\n   246\t\n   247\t**Evaluation criterion:** If a proof takes a \"minimal element U in the support of f\" (for example), it must verify that such a minimal element exists (the support is non-empty) and that minimality gives the claimed structure.\n   248\t\n   249\t### 7.4 Bijective Proofs\n   250\t\n   251\tA bijection between two sets proves they have the same cardinality. A bijective proof must:\n   252\t1. Define the map explicitly\n   253\t2. Show it is well-defined (maps valid objects to valid objects)\n   254\t3. Show it is injective (different inputs give different outputs)\n   255\t4. Show it is surjective (every valid output is achieved)\n   256\t\n   257\t**Alternative:** Show the map is a bijection by finding a two-sided inverse.\n   258\t\n   259\t---\n   260\t\n   261\t## Part VIII: Specific Problem Type Guidance\n   262\t\n   263\t### 8.1 Coloring Problems with Multiplicative Condition (proof_13 type)\n   264\t\n   265\t**Problem type:** Color subsets of S blue or red, count colorings where f(T₁)f(T₂) = f(T₁∪T₂)f(T₁∩T₂) for all T₁,T₂ ⊆ S.\n   266\t\n   267\t**Standard case split:** f(∅) = 1 (empty set is blue) vs f(∅) = 0 (empty set is red).\n   268\t\n   269\t**Case 1 (f(∅)=1):**\n   270\t- For i ∉ T: apply functional condition with T₁ = {i}, T₂ = T to get f({i})·f(T) = f(T∪{i})·f(∅) = f(T∪{i})\n   271\t- This gives f(T∪{i}) = λᵢ·f(T) where λᵢ = f({i})\n   272\t- By induction: f(T) = Π_{i∈T} λᵢ = Π_{i∈T} f({i})\n   273\t- Möbius inversion then recovers the blue sets exactly as subsets of A = {i : λᵢ = 2}\n   274\t- Converse: verify that coloring B = P(A) satisfies the multiplicativity for all T₁, T₂\n   275\t\n   276\t**Critical verification in Case 1 (often skipped):** The Möbius inversion step gives the blue set indicator, but one must verify that this formula gives 0 or 1 (not other values), and that the resulting B satisfies the original multiplicativity. This computation is:\n   277\t\n   278\t1_B(T) = Σ_{U⊆T} (-1)^{|T|-|U|} f(U) = Σ_{U⊆T} (-1)^{|T|-|U|} · 2^{|U∩A|}\n   279\t\n   280\tLet T∩A = T_A and T\\A = T_B. For U ⊆ T: U = U_A ∪ U_B with U_A ⊆ T_A and U_B ⊆ T_B. Then:\n   281\t\n   282\t1_B(T) = Σ_{U_A⊆T_A, U_B⊆T_B} (-1)^{|T_A|+|T_B|-|U_A|-|U_B|} · 2^{|U_A|}\n   283\t        = [Σ_{U_A⊆T_A} (-1)^{|T_A|-|U_A|} 2^{|U_A|}] · [Σ_{U_B⊆T_B} (-1)^{|T_B|-|U_B|}]\n   284\t\n   285\tThe second factor = Σ_{k=0}^{|T_B|} C(|T_B|,k)(-1)^{|T_B|-k} = (1-1)^{|T_B|} = 0^{|T_B|}.\n   286\t\n   287\tSo 1_B(T) = 0 if |T_B| > 0, i.e., if T ⊄ A.\n   288\tIf T ⊆ A, then T_B = ∅, so 1_B(T) = Σ_{U_A⊆T_A} (-1)^{|T_A|-|U_A|} 2^{|U_A|} = (2-1)^{|T_A|} = 1.\n   289\t\n   290\tSo 1_B(T) = 1 if T ⊆ A and 0 otherwise, i.e., B = P(A). ✓\n   291\t\n   292\t**This computation verifies Case 1.** A proof that only claims \"one checks\" without this computation has a minor omission (the computation is straightforward).\n   293\t\n   294\t**Case 2 (f(∅)=0):** \n   295\t- Functional condition with T₂ disjoint from T₁: f(T₁)f(T₂) = f(T₁∪T₂)·f(∅) = 0\n   296\t- So for any T₁, T₂ in the support of f, T₁∩T₂ ≠ ∅ (support is intersecting)\n   297\t- Take minimal element U in support; show f(U) = 1, B∩P(U) = {U}, support = {T : T⊇U}\n   298\t- Reduce to Case 1 on S' = S\\U via g(W) = f(U∪W)\n   299\t- Converse: verify that any interval [U, U∪A'] satisfies multiplicativity for ALL pairs T₁, T₂ ⊆ S\n   300\t\n   301\t**Critical gap in Case 2 converse:** If the proof claims \"one checks that [U, U∪A'] satisfies the condition\" without doing the check, and the check is non-trivial, this is a critical skipped step.\n   302\t\n   303\t**The check:** For B = [U, U∪A'], we have f(T) = g(T\\U)·χ_{T⊇U}(T) where g(W) = 2^{|W∩A'|}. For arbitrary T₁, T₂ ⊆ S:\n   304\t- If T₁ ⊅ U or T₂ ⊅ U: then at least one of f(T₁), f(T₂) equals 0. Also T₁∪T₂ may or may not ⊇ U, and T₁∩T₂ ⊇ U only if both T₁,T₂ ⊇ U (which fails). So at least one of f(T₁∪T₂), f(T₁∩T₂) = 0. The condition 0·f(T₂) = f(T₁∪T₂)·0 becomes 0 = 0 or 0 = 0. ✓\n   305\t- If T₁ ⊇ U and T₂ ⊇ U: then T₁∪T₂ ⊇ U and T₁∩T₂ ⊇ U. Let W₁ = T₁\\U, W₂ = T₂\\U. Then f(Tᵢ) = g(Wᵢ) = 2^{|Wᵢ∩A'|} and similarly. The condition becomes g(W₁)g(W₂) = g(W₁∪W₂)g(W₁∩W₂), i.e., 2^{|W₁∩A'|}·2^{|W₂∩A'|} = 2^{|(W₁∪W₂)∩A'|}·2^{|(W₁∩W₂)∩A'|}. This holds because |W₁∩A'|+|W₂∩A'| = |(W₁∩A')∪(W₂∩A')|+|(W₁∩A')∩(W₂∩A')| = |(W₁∪W₂)∩A'|+|(W₁∩W₂)∩A'| (using |A|+|B| = |A∪B|+|A∩B| for any finite sets). ✓\n   306\t\n   307\tSo the converse IS valid and checkable — but it requires this computation. The proof's \"one checks by routine verification\" is in fact routine (as shown above). However, an auditor must distinguish between (a) \"the proof says one checks and the check is indeed routine/trivial\" (= minor omission, proof essentially correct) and (b) \"the proof says one checks and the check is non-trivial and was actually skipped\" (= critical gap).\n   308\t\n   309\t**Verdict guidance for this problem type:** If the converse verification is ONLY cited as \"one checks\" and similar phrases appear at multiple critical steps, the cumulative effect of all the skipped steps determines the verdict.\n   310\t\n   311\t### 8.2 Grid/Matrix Set Systems with Monotonicity (proof_15 type)\n   312\t\n   313\t**Problem type:** Count collections {S_{ij}} with |S_{ij}| = i+j and S_{ij} ⊆ S_{kl} when i≤k, j≤l.\n   314\t\n   315\t**Standard reduction:** Write A_i = S_{i,0} and B_j = S_{0,j}. By monotonicity:\n   316\t- A_i = S_{i,0} ⊆ S_{i,j}\n   317\t- B_j = S_{0,j} ⊆ S_{i,j}\n   318\t- Hence A_i ∪ B_j ⊆ S_{i,j}\n   319\t- |A_i∪B_j| ≤ |A_i|+|B_j| = i+j = |S_{ij}|\n   320\t\n   321\tThe conclusion S_{ij} = A_i ∪ B_j requires |A_i∪B_j| = i+j, which requires A_i ∩ B_j = ∅.\n   322\t\n   323\t**Critical point:** The argument \"|A_i∪B_j| ≤ i+j = |S_{ij}| implies S_{ij} = A_i∪B_j\" is INVALID without additionally knowing |A_i∪B_j| = i+j. The bound |A_i∪B_j| ≤ i+j combined with A_i∪B_j ⊆ S_{ij} and |S_{ij}| = i+j shows A_i∪B_j ⊆ S_{ij} with |A_i∪B_j| ≤ |S_{ij}|. But this does NOT imply equality — it only shows A_i∪B_j could be a proper subset of S_{ij}.\n   324\t\n   325\tTo CORRECTLY conclude S_{ij} = A_i∪B_j, one needs |A_i∪B_j| = i+j, which requires A_i∩B_j = ∅. But A_i∩B_j = ∅ is derived from S_{ij} = A_i∪B_j! This is CIRCULAR.\n   326\t\n   327\tA correct proof would avoid this circularity by first establishing A_n∩B_n = ∅ (using |S_{nn}| = 2n and A_n∪B_n ⊆ S_{nn}, with |A_n|+|B_n| = 2n, forcing |A_n∩B_n| = 0 IF |S_{nn}| = |A_n∪B_n| = 2n). But even this requires |S_{nn}| = |A_n∪B_n|, which again needs A_n∩B_n = ∅...\n   328\t\n   329\tThe correct resolution: since A_n∪B_n ⊆ S_{nn} and |S_{nn}| = 2n and |A_n|+|B_n| = n+n = 2n, we need |A_n∪B_n| ≤ 2n (trivially true). But for a proper subset A_n∪B_n ⊊ S_{nn}, we'd need |A_n∪B_n| < 2n, i.e., A_n∩B_n ≠ ∅. Conversely, S_{nn} ⊆ {1,...,2n} with |S_{nn}| = 2n means S_{nn} = {1,...,2n}. And |A_n∪B_n| ≤ |{1,...,2n}| = 2n with |A_n|+|B_n| = 2n implies |A_n∩B_n| ≥ 0, so possibly A_n∩B_n ≠ ∅.\n   330\t\n   331\tActually, since A_n∪B_n ⊆ S_{nn} = {1,...,2n} (ALL elements), we have |A_n∪B_n| ≤ 2n. And |A_n|+|B_n| = 2n. So |A_n∩B_n| = |A_n|+|B_n|-|A_n∪B_n| ≥ 2n-2n = 0. This gives no contradiction.\n   332\t\n   333\tThe proof IS circular unless there's additional structure. The correct proof would use a different approach (e.g., induction or direct construction from disjoint chains).\n   334\t\n   335\t### 8.3 Sum Optimization with Distinct Integers (proof_19 type)\n   336\t\n   337\t**Problem type:** Find minimum N such that there exist 2k+1 distinct positive integers with total sum > N but every k-element subset sums to ≤ N/2.\n   338\t\n   339\t**Key structural observations:**\n   340\t1. The condition \"every k-subset ≤ N/2\" is most constraining for the largest k elements\n   341\t2. The condition \"total sum > N\" is most constraining for the smallest k+1 elements\n   342\t3. A symmetric set around some center c is a natural candidate for the extremal configuration\n   343\t\n   344\t**Construction technique:** Take S = {c-k, c-k+1, ..., c, ..., c+k} for some c.\n   345\t- |S| = 2k+1 ✓ (distinct positive integers if c > k)\n   346\t- Largest k elements: {c+1,...,c+k}, sum = kc+k(k+1)/2\n   347\t- Set N = 2·(kc+k(k+1)/2) to make the largest k-subset sum exactly N/2\n   348\t- Then total sum = (2k+1)c + 0 = ? No, total = Σ_{j=-k}^k (c+j) = (2k+1)c\n   349\t\n   350\tWait: Σ_{j=-k}^k (c+j) = (2k+1)c + Σ_{j=-k}^k j = (2k+1)c + 0 = (2k+1)c.\n   351\t\n   352\tAnd N = 2(kc + k(k+1)/2) = 2kc + k(k+1).\n   353\t\n   354\tFor total sum > N: (2k+1)c > 2kc+k(k+1) iff c > k(k+1), i.e., c ≥ k(k+1)+1.\n   355\t\n   356\tWith c = k(k+1)+1: N = 2k·(k(k+1)+1)+k(k+1) = 2k²(k+1)+2k+k(k+1) = k(2k+3)(k+1)+2k... let me compute directly.\n   357\t\n   358\tc = k(k+1)+1 = k²+k+1.\n   359\tN = 2kc+k(k+1) = 2k(k²+k+1)+k(k+1) = 2k³+2k²+2k+k²+k = 2k³+3k²+3k ✓\n   360\t\n   361\tTotal sum = (2k+1)c = (2k+1)(k²+k+1).\n   362\t\n   363\tNeed: (2k+1)(k²+k+1) > 2k³+3k²+3k.\n   364\t\n   365\t(2k+1)(k²+k+1) = 2k³+2k²+2k+k²+k+1 = 2k³+3k²+3k+1. ✓ (Exceeds N=2k³+3k²+3k by 1.)\n   366\t\n   367\t**Minimality argument:** Must show N < 2k³+3k²+3k leads to contradiction. Set a_{k+1} = c (the (k+1)-th smallest element in sorted order). Using a_{k+1+j} ≥ c+j and a_{k+1-j} ≤ c-j (both strict since elements are distinct integers), derive c ≥ k(k+1)+1, hence S_k ≥ N/2.\n   368\t\n   369\t**Evaluation criterion for proof_19 type:** The proof must:\n   370\t(a) Correctly compute N = 2k³+3k²+3k\n   371\t(b) Exhibit the construction explicitly\n   372\t(c) Verify both sum conditions for the construction\n   373\t(d) Prove minimality through a general lower bound\n   374\t\n   375\tAny error in (a)-(d) is a material issue.\n   376\t\n   377\t### 8.4 Non-General Construction Problems (proof_12 type)\n   378\t\n   379\t**Problem type:** Construct a combinatorial object (e.g., a graph or set system) with given properties.\n   380\t\n   381\t**Common gap:** Proving existence for specific small cases (n=1,2,3) and claiming the general case follows \"by analogous construction\" without providing the general construction.\n   382\t\n   383\t**Evaluation criterion:** If the proof only handles specific cases, the verdict should be incorrect if the problem asks for a GENERAL construction. A construction that works for specific n values but doesn't generalize is not a valid proof of the general claim.\n   384\t\n   385\t---\n   386\t\n   387\t## Part IX: Combinatorics Proof Evaluation Rubric\n   388\t\n   389\t1. **Completeness of case analysis:** All cases must be covered. Especially: if splitting on f(∅) = 0 vs f(∅) = 1, both cases must be handled.\n   390\t\n   391\t2. **Converse direction:** When classifying all valid configurations, both directions of \"if and only if\" must be proved. A proof that only shows (⇒) has a gap.\n   392\t\n   393\t3. **Counting formula correctness:** Verify every counting formula. C(2n,n)·n!·n! = (2n)! by C(2n,n) = (2n)!/(n!·n!), multiplied by n!·n! = (2n)!/1 = (2n)!. ✓\n   394\t\n   395\t4. **Non-circularity:** The reduction to boundary chains must not assume what it is trying to prove. Establishing A_i∩B_j = ∅ requires a separate non-circular argument.\n   396\t\n   397\t5. **Generality:** Does the proof work for general n (or k, or S), not just specific values?\n   398\t\n   399\t6. **Verification of construction:** Every claimed construction must be verified to satisfy ALL the problem's conditions.\n   400\t\n   401\t7. **Skipped steps:** \"One checks\" and \"by routine verification\" are acceptable for genuinely routine computations (like Möbius inversion in specific cases), but NOT for non-trivial verifications. Assess whether the skipped step is truly routine or not.\n   402\t\n   403\t**Identifying the first material issue:** Work through the proof sequentially. The FIRST step that violates one of criteria 1-7 is the first material issue.\n   404\t\n   405\t\n   406\t---\n   407\t\n   408\t## Appendix A: Extended Examples for Combinatorics Proof Evaluation\n   409\t\n   410\t### A.1 The Coloring Classification Problem (proof_13 type) — Step-by-Step Audit Guide\n   411\t\n   412\t**Complete structure of valid colorings:**\n   413\t\n   414\tA coloring (B, set of blue subsets) satisfies f(T1)f(T2)=f(T1 cup T2)f(T1 cap T2) for all T1,T2 in P(S) iff B belongs to one of these families:\n   415\t\n   416\tFamily 1: B = P(A) for some A subset S (includes empty set = A=empty gives B={empty set})\n   417\tFamily 2: B = [U, U cup A'] for some nonempty U subset S and A' subset S\\U (interval in Boolean lattice)\n   418\tFamily 3: B = empty (all-red, the \"zero\" solution)\n   419\t\n   420\tNote: Family 3 corresponds to f equiv 0 which vacuously satisfies the equation.\n   421\tFamily 1 corresponds to f(empty)=1.\n   422\tFamily 2 corresponds to f(empty)=0 with some blue set.\n   423\t\n   424\t**Count:** |Family 1| = 2^n (one per A subset S). |Family 2| = sum_{nonempty U} 2^{n-|U|} = 3^n-2^n. |Family 3| = 1.\n   425\tTotal = 2^n + (3^n - 2^n) + 1 = 3^n + 1.\n   426\t\n   427\t**First audit step:** Verify the proof splits into f(empty)=1 vs f(empty)=0 cases.\n   428\t**Second audit step:** Verify Case 1 concludes B=P(A) for 2^n colorings.\n   429\t**Third audit step:** Verify Case 2 concludes B=[U, U cup A'] plus all-red for 3^n-2^n+1 colorings.\n   430\t**Fourth audit step:** Add: 2^n + 3^n - 2^n + 1 = 3^n + 1.\n   431\t\n   432\tAny error in these four steps is a material issue.\n   433\t\n   434\t### A.2 The S_{ij} Counting Problem (proof_15 type) — Audit Guide\n   435\t\n   436\t**Setup:** Count valid {S_{ij}: 0<=i,j<=n, S_{ij} subset {1,...,2n}} with |S_{ij}|=i+j and S_{ij} subset S_{kl} when i<=k, j<=l.\n   437\t\n   438\t**The claimed reduction:** Every valid collection {S_{ij}} arises from a pair of disjoint chains A_0 subset ... subset A_n and B_0 subset ... subset B_n with A_n cap B_n = empty, via S_{ij} = A_i cup B_j.\n   439\t\n   440\t**Circularity check:** The proof must establish A_i cap B_j = empty BEFORE claiming S_{ij} = A_i cup B_j. If it first claims equality then derives empty intersection, it is circular.\n   441\t\n   442\t**Non-circular approach outline:**\n   443\tStep 1: From A_n cup B_n subset S_{nn} and |S_{nn}|=2n, we get S_{nn}={1,...,2n}.\n   444\tStep 2: From inclusion-exclusion |A_n cup B_n| = |A_n|+|B_n|-|A_n cap B_n| = 2n-|A_n cap B_n|.\n   445\tStep 3: But A_n cup B_n is not necessarily {1,...,2n}; we only know it's a subset of size <=2n.\n   446\tStep 4: (Stuck — the above doesn't give A_n cap B_n = empty without additional work.)\n   447\t\n   448\tA correct proof uses a different approach, such as:\n   449\t- Working with the largest sets and their complements\n   450\t- Using induction on n\n   451\t- Constructing the chains directly from a valid {S_{ij}} by setting A_i = S_{i,0} and showing these form a chain (obvious from i<=k => S_{i,0} subset S_{k,0}) and similarly for B, then establishing disjointness by a non-circular argument\n   452\t\n   453\tThe specific difficulty: establishing S_{ij}=A_i cup B_j requires |A_i cup B_j|=i+j, which requires disjointness, which the proof assumes from the conclusion. This circularity is a first material issue in this type of proof: the characterization is unproven.\n   454\t\n   455\t### A.3 Combinatorial Optimization (proof_19 type) — Detailed Audit\n   456\t\n   457\t**Problem:** Find minimum N such that there exist 2k+1 distinct positive integers with sum > N and every k-subset sums to at most N/2.\n   458\t\n   459\t**Key lemmas:**\n   460\t\n   461\tLemma 1 (Largest k-subset): The maximum sum of a k-element subset of {a_1 < ... < a_{2k+1}} is a_{k+2} + ... + a_{2k+1} (the k largest elements).\n   462\tProof: Any k elements sum to at most the k largest. ✓\n   463\t\n   464\tLemma 2 (Lower bound on c): With c = a_{k+1} and the conditions, c >= k(k+1)+1.\n   465\tProof: From sum_{i=1}^{k+1} a_i > S_k (where S_k = sum of k largest):\n   466\t  sum_{i=1}^k a_i + c > S_k >= kc + k(k+1)/2\n   467\t  But sum_{i=1}^k a_i <= kc - k(k+1)/2 (since a_{k+1-j} <= c-j for j=1,...,k)\n   468\t  So kc - k(k+1)/2 >= sum_{i=1}^k a_i > kc + k(k+1)/2 - c = (k-1)c + k(k+1)/2\n   469\t  This gives kc - k(k+1)/2 > (k-1)c + k(k+1)/2\n   470\t  c > k(k+1). So c >= k(k+1)+1. ✓\n   471\t\n   472\tLemma 3 (Minimum N): N' >= 2k^3+3k^2+3k.\n   473\tProof: N' >= 2S_k >= 2(kc + k(k+1)/2) >= 2(k(k(k+1)+1) + k(k+1)/2) = 2k^2(k+1)+2k + k(k+1) = 2k^3+3k^2+3k. ✓\n   474\t\n   475\tLemma 4 (Construction achieves N=2k^3+3k^2+3k): Set c=k(k+1)+1, S={c-k,...,c+k}.\n   476\t- k-subset max sum: kc+k(k+1)/2 = N/2. ✓ (all k-subsets have sum <= N/2)\n   477\t- Total sum: (2k+1)c = N+1 > N. ✓\n   478\t\n   479\t**Evaluation criterion:** A proof correctly establishing Lemmas 1-4 is CORRECT. The first error in any lemma is the first material issue.\n   480\t\n   481\t### A.4 Non-General Construction Problem (proof_12 type) — Audit Guide\n   482\t\n   483\t**Common structure of proof_12 type problems:** \"Construct [combinatorial object with property P for all n >= 1].\"\n   484\t\n   485\t**Required proof elements:**\n   486\t1. Description of a construction (for general n)\n   487\t2. Verification that the construction has property P\n   488\t\n   489\t**Common gap:** Construction given for small n (e.g., n=1,2,3) with \"similar for larger n\" or \"obvious generalization.\" Without specifying the general construction, this is incomplete.\n   490\t\n   491\t**Evaluation criterion:**\n   492\t- If the proof ONLY handles specific small cases: INCORRECT (first material issue = missing general construction)\n   493\t- If the proof gives a general construction but fails to verify P: INCORRECT (first material issue = missing verification)\n   494\t- If both are present: evaluate whether the verification is correct\n   495\t\n   496\t---\n   497\t\n   498\t## Appendix B: Additional Combinatorial Identities and Techniques\n   499\t\n   500\t### B.1 Key Subset Sum Identities\n   501\t\n   502\tFor S = {1,...,n} and A subset S with |A|=k:\n   503\tsum_{A subset S, |A|=k} sum_{i in A} i = C(n-1,k-1) * sum_{i=1}^n i = C(n-1,k-1) * n(n+1)/2\n   504\t\n   505\tThis counts pairs (i, A) with i in A and |A|=k: each i is in C(n-1,k-1) such sets.\n   506\t\n   507\t### B.2 Principle of Inclusion-Exclusion — Extended\n   508\t\n   509\tFor events A_1,...,A_m in sample space Omega:\n   510\tP(A_1 cup ... cup A_m) = sum P(A_i) - sum_{i<j} P(A_i cap A_j) + ... + (-1)^{m+1} P(A_1 cap ... cap A_m)\n   511\t\n   512\t**Sieve formula:** Number of elements with NO property = sum_{S subset [m]} (-1)^|S| * |cap_{i in S} A_i|\n   513\t\n   514\t**Derangement formula:** D(n) = n! * sum_{k=0}^n (-1)^k / k!\n   515\t\n   516\t### B.3 Lattice Path Counting\n   517\t\n   518\tA lattice path from (0,0) to (m,n) using steps (1,0) and (0,1) has C(m+n,m) total paths.\n   519\t\n   520\t**Ballot problem:** Path from (0,0) to (m,n) with m>n that stays strictly above the diagonal: C(m+n,m) * (m-n)/(m+n).\n   521\t\n   522\t**Catalan numbers:** C_n = C(2n,n)/(n+1) counts Dyck paths (lattice paths from (0,0) to (2n,0) with steps +1,-1 staying >= 0).\n   523\t\n   524\t### B.4 Counting Chains in Boolean Lattice\n   525\t\n   526\tNumber of chains of length k+1 (k+1 elements including empty) in B_n:\n   527\t= sum over A_0 subset A_1 subset ... subset A_k (each proper containment and in S)\n   528\t= n! / (n-k)! * C(n,k) ... (complex formula)\n   529\t\n   530\tFor maximal chains (length n+1): exactly n! (orderings of S).\n   531\t\n   532\tFor chains A_0 subset A_1 subset ... subset A_n with |A_i|=i:\n   533\t= n! (one for each ordering/labeling of S as {s_1,...,s_n}, taking A_i = {s_1,...,s_i})\n   534\t\n   535\tThis is the bijection: chains of this type <-> permutations of S. Hence n! chains.\n   536\t\n   537\t### B.5 Counting Disjoint Chains\n   538\t\n   539\tTwo disjoint chains in B_{2n}: choose A-chain of length n+1 and B-chain of length n+1 with A_n cap B_n = empty.\n   540\t\n   541\tMethod 1 (direct count):\n   542\tChoose A_n: C(2n,n) ways. B_n = complement of A_n: 1 way.\n   543\tChoose chain inside A_n: n! ways (orderings of A_n).\n   544\tChoose chain inside B_n: n! ways.\n   545\tTotal: C(2n,n) * n! * n! = (2n)!/(n!*n!) * n!^2 = (2n)!.\n   546\t\n   547\tMethod 2 (permutation bijection): Each pair of disjoint chains (A, B) of length n+1 with A_n cup B_n = {1,...,2n} corresponds to a permutation sigma of {1,...,2n}: put sigma(1),...,sigma(n) in A-chain order and sigma(n+1),...,sigma(2n) in B-chain order. There are (2n)! permutations, giving (2n)! pairs. ✓\n   548\t\n   549\t---\n   550\t\n   551\t## Appendix C: Worked Derivations for Counting Arguments\n   552\t\n   553\t### C.1 Sum Σ_{k=1}^n C(n,k) * 2^{n-k} = 3^n - 2^n\n   554\t\n   555\tProof: sum_{k=0}^n C(n,k) * 2^{n-k} = sum_{k=0}^n C(n,k) * 2^{n-k} * 1^k = (2+1)^n = 3^n by binomial theorem.\n   556\tSubtract k=0 term: C(n,0)*2^n = 2^n.\n   557\tSo sum_{k=1}^n C(n,k)*2^{n-k} = 3^n - 2^n. ✓\n   558\t\n   559\t### C.2 Total Case 2 Count = 1 + (3^n - 2^n) = 3^n - 2^n + 1\n   560\t\n   561\tAs computed: Case 2 gives all-red (1 coloring) plus B=[U,U cup A'] for each nonempty U and each A' subset S\\U.\n   562\tFor fixed |U|=m: C(n,m) choices for U, 2^{n-m} choices for A'. So contributions: sum_{m=1}^n C(n,m)*2^{n-m} = 3^n-2^n.\n   563\tTotal: 1 + (3^n-2^n) = 3^n - 2^n + 1. ✓\n   564\t\n   565\t### C.3 Verification: 2^n + (3^n-2^n+1) = 3^n+1\n   566\t\n   567\t2^n + 3^n - 2^n + 1 = 3^n + 1. ✓ The 2^n terms cancel.\n   568\t\n   569\t### C.4 Minimality N = 2k^3+3k^2+3k — Computation Check\n   570\t\n   571\tFor k=1: N = 2+3+3 = 8. Max 3-subset sum=14+15+16=45=N/2. ✓\n   574\t\n   575\t### C.5 Counting Disjoint Chains for Small n\n   576\t\n   577\tn=1: Choose A_1 (1 element from {1,2}): C(2,1)=2 ways. B_1=complement: 1 way.
